    rev 0.1-alpha

    A General Theory of Data Access

    In order to access data, it is necessary to understand the geometry of data. That is, the extension or shape of the data. However, the shape we perceive is only tangentially related to the data itself. For example, three people looking at the same table in a room will see three completely different objects. To the first person, floating high above, pinned to the ceiling, the table appears to be a large rectangle. The second person, whose eyes are level with the table, also sees a rectangle, but one that approaches much more of a line, then a large bulky block. And the last, eye level as well, but 90 degrees shifted around the side sees a line-ish rectangle as well, but one that is much smaller than the previous participant’s description.

    The function and purpose of perspective (more generally, the reason for the existence of agenthood in this reality we find ourselves moored to) is made apparent when one wishes to look at data --- a metaphor appropriate for humans, where the more general act is that of accessing data). While the reader searches for some unbeknownst depth in the previous sentence, a sleight of hands on the author’s part and another sort of table emerges which has our participants expressing nearly identical descriptions to those for when the "table" was in the room.

    ceiling view eye level (long-wise) eye level (short-wise) 
    { [name=>marko,age=>29],
  [name=>josh,age=>32],
  [name=>vadas,age=>27],
  [name=>peter,age=>35] }
    		 
    { [name=>marko],
  [name=>josh],
  [name=>vadas],
  [name=>peter] }
    		 
    {
  [name=>marko, age=>29]

}

    Instructions

    • inst uri syntax
    • obj propagation mechanics
    • stacks and frames
    • dom/rng coefficients

    mtron programs are composed using computational primitives called instructions (inst). Instructions are organized into libraries called instruction sets (instset) — domains of discourse. Mathematically, every instruction is a function that maps a multiset of objs to a multiset of objs — called streams.

    \[ f\left(\cal{P}\left(\tt{X}\right)\right) \rightarrow \cal{P}\left(\tt{Y}\right) \]

    An instruction maps a left hand side obj (lhs) to a right hand side obj (rhs). One-to-one, many-to-one, one-to-many, many-to-many, etc. mappings are possible as domains and ranges are specified as elements of the power set of all objs. When coefficients are elements of \(\mathbb{Z}\), {1,2,3} \(\in \mathcal{P}(\texttt{obj})\) has type int{3}. Programmatically, what is passed between instructions is a single stream of objs. This formulation guarantees that there is always a rhs obj for every lhs obj regardless of failure (fail::T) or emptiness (obj{0}).

    As a side note, discussed at length elsewhere, the mtron inst ring is the set of objs, closed under \(\cdot\) (apply) and \(+\) (split/merge), both of which are total functions.

    inst uri syntax

    In mtron, instructions are written according to the form:

     tid{c}?rng<=dom(args){ code }@vid
  │  │  │      │ │  │  │    │   │
  │  │  └───┬──┘ └─┬┘  └─┬──┘   │
  │  │      │      │     │      └────⮞ value id: the position of the instruction in code
  │  │      │      │     └───────────⮞ code: instruction body is a tree of instructions (impl)
  │  │      │      └─────────────────⮞ arguments: dynamic parameterizations (args)
  │  │      └────────────────────────⮞ domain/range: the incoming (lhs) and outgoing (rhs) obj types
  │  └───────────────────────────────⮞ coefficient: the magnitude of the instruction upon the rhs
  └──────────────────────────────────⮞ type id: the type of the instruction (opcode)

    The instruction template above represents the typical instruction with rng<=dom and {c} sugar syntax being used. However, note that the dom/rng and coefficient sugars ultimately compile to URI query parameters. When fully parsed to a legal URI, the instruction signature is denoted:

    inst?dom=a&rng=b&c=x,y

    Instruction query parameters are similar in function to method annotations. Query parameters encode metadata which can aid the compiler instruction selection, enable code path compression, facilitate polymorphic dispatch, support lifted monadic processing, maintain runtime profiling statistics, and more.

    obj propagation mechanics

    It is important to understand the mechanics of instruction evaluation. Every instruction follows the same pattern. When a lhs obj is applied to an inst obj, the first thing that happens is the arguments of the inst are resolved. This is perhaps best described with an example.

    mtron> 1.plus(2)     ​1​
==>3
mtron> 1.plus(id())     ​2​
==>2
mtron> 1.plus(plus(2))     ​3​
==>4
mtron> 1.map(matches(bool::T))     ​4​
==>false
mtron> 1.map(isa(bool::T))     ​5​
mtron> 1.as(bool::T)     ​6​
==>true
mtron> 1.isa(as(bool::T))     ​7​
==>1
    1 1 is applied to 2 and then 1 is added to 2 to yield 3.
    2 1 is applied to the identity function to yield 1 and then 1 is added to 1 to yield 2.
    3 1 is applied to the instruction plus(2) to yield 3 and then 1 is added to 3 to yield 4.
    4 1 is applied to the instruction matches(bool::T) to yield and then emit bool::false.
    5 1 is applied to the instruction isa(bool::T) to yield and then emit noobj (i.e. nothing).
    6 1 is applied to the instruction as() to emit bool::true (0 would emit bool::false).
    7 1 is applied to the instruction as(bool::T) to yield true and then is applied to isa to allow 1 to pass unfiltered (i.e. not nothing).

    The key point is that call objs (inst, code, and noobj) have a domain and range that are context dependent as each incoming obj creates a chain reaction of internal calls to ultimately yield the arg objs that instruction will operate with. Said another way, calls are types that become instances at time of application.

    The diagram below demonstrates how a lhs obj percolates through an inst to where ultimately, at the end of the last inst of code the resultant rhs obj is emitted.

         ┌────────────────┐
 ┌───┴───────┐        │       ┌──┐     ┌────────┐
 │           ▼ 1      ▼ 2     │  ▼ 4   │      6 ▼
lhs => inst(arg1,...,argN){ inst1...instN } => rhs
 │                          3 ▲   │  ▲ 5
 └────────────────────────────┘   └──┘
    the unification of function and value

    Constant objs (non-call objs) also have a domain and range. For instance, the verbose representation of int::1 is <1>?int<=#{?}(){1}.

    mtron> |<1>?int<=#{?}(){1}@<1>   [-- 1 --]
==>1
mtron> <1>()                     [-- 2 --]
mtron> 10.<1>()                  [-- 3 --]
==>fail::[unable to determine inst function:
   	10          => 1?dom=int()@<1>   | [inst]
   	int::T      => int::T   |  \_dom
   	int::T     ==> [,]   |  \_args]@/sys/fail/180
mtron> 12 + <1>()                [-- 4 --]
==>fail::[apply failure:
   	[lhs]    │ 12
   	 \_type  │ /m/int
   	  \_pred │ []
   	[inst]   │ plus?rng=int&dom=int(fail::[unable to determine inst function:]@/sys/fail/184){<j>}@(1)
   	 \_dom   │ int::T
   	 \_args  │ [fail::[unable to determine inst function:]@/sys/fail/186][MTronException<137>:fail::[unable to determine inst function:...]][fail::[unable to determine inst function:
   	12          => 1?dom=int()   | [inst]
   	int::T      => int::T   |  \_dom
   	int::T     ==> [,]   |  \_args]@/sys/fail/182 [value] unable to convert fail::T]@/sys/fail/188
    1 1 as an inst from maybe an obj to an int (| to prevent evaluation).
    2 noobj applied to the 1 inst (the {0} of the domain’s {?}).
    3 10 applied to the 1 inst (the {1} of the domain’s {?}).
    4 12 added to the 1 inst.

    This states that every lhs obj or noobj (#{?}) that is applied to 1, yields a single rhs int; namely 1. In this way, constants are functions (calls) even though they return the same value regardless of input.

    stacks and frames

    Every logical thread of execution has a /m/space/stack patterned <+/#>. This pattern captures the breadth of common variable names found in most programming languages (a,x,a_var,b3,etc.) as well as hierarchical variables like a/b/c, etc. In short, the stack is always responsible for schemaless relative uris. To ground the discussion, the current stack of the mtron code processing this documentation is shown in the collapsed accordion below. The state of this stack is the result of all the processing thus far up to this point in the documentation. Declared variables from other parts of the documentation will be noticed as they were written at the root of the stack and thus, are global variables. Furthermore, note the boot/ branch. This stores the data provided by the user and/or derived by the bootloader at startup (e.g. environmental variables).

    the objs of the doc processors stack 
    mtron> *<+/#/>
==>boot/args/boot=></home/killswitch/software/metatron/boot/docs.mtron>
==>boot/args=>[
    log=>info,
    boot=></home/killswitch/software/metatron/boot/docs.mtron>,
    local=>booger]
==><1/x/total>=>15.0000
==>v5/name=>'ripple'
==>chicken=>animal{*}::T@chicken
==>boot/args/local=>booger
==>animal=>rec::T[?[name=>str::T,age=>int::T]]@animal
==>v6/age=>35
==><1/x>=>[
    item=>'book',
    total=>15.0000,
    seller=>!*g:V/4]@<1/x>
==><1>=>plus?rng=int&dom=int(fail::[unable to determine inst function:]@/sys/fail/190){<j>}@(1)
==>v5/lang=>java
==>knows=>edge::T[?[weight=>real::T]]@knows
==>person=>vrtx::T[?[name=>str::T,age=>int::T]]@person
==>animals=>rec::T[?[
     __mtron_tid=>str::T,
     name=>str::T,
     age=>int::T,
     {?}shelter=>rec::T]]@animals
==>v1/outE_=>[knows=>{[weight=>0.5000,inV_=>!*v2,outV_=>!*v1],[weight=>0.5000,inV_=>!*v4,outV_=>!*v1]}]
==>v2/name=>'vadas'
==>v1/created=>[weight=>0.2000,inV_=>!*v3,outV_=>!*v1]
==>v4=>[name=>'lop']
==>v1=>[
    name=>'marko',
    age=>29,
    outE_=>[knows=>{[weight=>0.5000,inV_=>!*v2,outV_=>!*v1],[weight=>0.5000,inV_=>!*v4,outV_=>!*v1]}],
    created=>[weight=>0.2000,inV_=>!*v3,outV_=>!*v1]]
==>v6/out_=>[created=>!*v4]
==>v1/name=>'marko'
==>boot/file=></home/killswitch/software/metatron/boot/docs.mtron>@boot/file
==>equipment=>rec{*}::T[?[model=>uri::T,year=>int::T]]@equipment
==><1/x/item>=>'book'
==>v6=>[
    name=>'peter',
    age=>35,
    out_=>[created=>!*v4]]
==>v2=>[name=>'vadas']
==>v4/name=>'lop'
==>v1/age=>29
==>boot/args/log=>info
==>v3=>[name=>'josh']
==>v6/name=>'peter'
==>software=>vrtx::T[?[name=>str::T,lang=>str::T]]@software
==><1/x/seller>=>!*g:V/4
==>v3/name=>'josh'
==>created=>edge{*}::T[?[weight=>real::T]]@created
==>out=>out?rng=rec{*}&dom=rec(label=>uri{*}::T){rshift(outE_).rshift(*label).rshift(inV_)}
==>v5=>[name=>'ripple',lang=>java]
     lhs ──⮞ inst(arg1,...,argN){ inst1...instN } ──⮞ rhs
  │           │            │  │             │
  │   push    └─────┬──────┘  └─────────────┘
┌─┼─────────────────┼──────┐     inst body        ┌─┐
│ │             inst│frame │                      │ │
│ ▼                 │      ├─────────────────────►│X│
│      stack        ▼      │          pop         │ │
└──────────────────────────┘                      └─┘

    When an instruction is called, the associated arguments have the lhs obj applied and the result of the application is pushed onto the stack — accessible through reference for the entirety of the inst code block. From this architecture, the following inst forms are made available for use.

    positional args
    \[\tt{obj}_\tt{lhs} \cdot f(\color{yellow}{\__0}, \color{yellow}{\__1},\ldots,\color{yellow}{\__n}) \mapsto \tt{obj}_\tt{rhs}\]

    The arguments are captured as a lst and are accessed via their position. Realize that <1> is uri::1, not int::1. *<0> references the first argument of the args lst, *<1> the second argument, and so forth. Note that for single digit numbers, a syntax sugar exists where the uri < > wrapper isn’t required and the position references can be written as *0, *1, etc.

    In the example below, the user defined instruction band filters out lhs objs not within the provided range (min/max). Given the filtering behavior of the instruction, the signature of band is int{?}<=int. That is, it accepts a single int and returns either noobj ({0}) or the int ({1}). Hence, a range of int{?}.

               *0                   *1
┌───────────┬────────────────────┬────────────┐
│XXXXXXXXXXX│                    │XXXXXXXXXXXX│
└───────────┴────────────────────┴────────────┘
    mtron> |inst?int{?}<=int(_,_){ is(and(gte(*0),lte(*1))) }@band     ​1​
mtron> *band     ​2​
mtron> 3.band(2,8)     ​3​
==>3
mtron> 10.band(2,8)     ​4​
    1 The | is the block instruction and is necessary so the inst isn’t evaluated but instead yielded as an inst.
    2 dereference the uri band to yield the inst definition.
    3 3 is within the band [2,8] and thus, 3 is the result, satisfying the inst range int{?}.
    4 10 is not within the band [2,8] and thus, the result is noobj which satisfies the inst range int{?}.
    named args
    \[\tt{obj}_\tt{lhs} \cdot f(\color{yellow}{a_0} \Rightarrow x, \color{yellow}{a_1} \Rightarrow y,\ldots,a_n) \mapsto \tt{obj}_\tt{rhs}\]

    When the arguments are provided in a rec form, the key of each relation of the rec is a named argument. Named arguments are resolved from the stack via their furi name.

                *min                  *max
┌────────────┬────────────────────┬────────────┐
│XXXXXXXXXXXX│                    │XXXXXXXXXXXX│
└────────────┴────────────────────┴────────────┘
    mtron> |inst?int{?}<=int(min=>_,max=>_){ is(and(gte(*min),lte(*max))) }@band
mtron> 3.band(min=>2,max=>4)     ​1​
==>3
mtron> 3.band(2,8)     ​2​
==>3
mtron> 3.band(max=>8,min=>2)     ​3​
==>3
mtron> 'abc'.band(2,8)     ​4​
    1 The argument’s name is it’s reference on the stack.
    2 If the argument name isn’t provided, then it’s position in the argument list determines its name.
    3 When arguments are named, they can be written in any order.
    4 The domain of band is int. Note where the error is thrown when str['abc'] is provided.
    default args
    \[\tt{obj}_\tt{lhs} \cdot f(a_0 \Rightarrow \color{yellow}{\tt{else}(x)},a_1 \Rightarrow \color{yellow}{\tt{else}(y)},\ldots,a_n) \mapsto \tt{obj}_\tt{rhs}\]

    The else instruction has numerous uses. With respect to instruction arguments, the else instruction provides the user the ability to express default arguments. If the incoming obj to else is noobj, then else returns it’s argument, otherwise it returns the incoming lhs obj.

            *min | 2             *max | 8
┌────────────┬────────────────────┬────────────┐
│XXXXXXXXXXXX│                    │XXXXXXXXXXXX│
└────────────┴────────────────────┴────────────┘
    mtron> |inst?int{?}<=int(min=>else(2),max=>else(8)){ is(and(gte(*min),lte(*max))) }@band
mtron> 1.band(min=>1)     ​1​
==>1
mtron> 10.band()     ​2​
mtron> {2,3,4,5}.band(min=>3,max=>4)     ​3​
==>3
==>4
    1 No max is provided, so the default value of 8 is used.
    2 No min nor max is provided, so the defaults 2 and 8, respectively are used.
    3 When arguments are provided, the default values are not used.
    typed args
    \[\tt{obj}_\tt{lhs} \cdot f(a_0 \Rightarrow \color{yellow}{?\tt{type}_0}, a_2 \Rightarrow \color{yellow}{?\tt{type}_1},\ldots,a_n \Rightarrow \color{yellow}{?\tt{type}_2}) \mapsto \tt{obj}_\tt{rhs}}\]

    The previous examples made use of the polymorphic id instruction (sugar’d _). It’s important to realize that an argument can be any arbitrary obj (thus, code). Type arguments are the means by which instruction arguments are typed.

                *min                 *max
┌────────────┬────────────────────┬────────────┐
│XXXXXXXXXXXX│                    │XXXXXXXXXXXX│
└────────────┴────────────────────┴────────────┘
            ?>0 ───────────────────────────────⮞
    mtron> nat  -> int::T[?>0]@nat     ​1​
mtron> |inst?int{?}<=int(min=>?nat::T,max=>?nat::T){ is(and(gte(*min),lte(*max))) }@band
mtron> 15.band(10,20)     ​2​
==>15
mtron> -15.band(-20,-10)     ​3​
==>-15
mtron> band -> |inst?int{?}<=int(min=>as(nat::T),max=>as(nat::T)){ is(and(gte(*min),lte(*max))) }
mtron> 15.band(10,20)     ​4​
==>15
mtron> -15.band(-20,-10)     ​5​
mtron> band -> |inst?int{?}<=int(min=>?nat::T.else(throw()),max=>?nat::T.else(throw())){ is(and(gte(*min),lte(*max))) }
mtron> 15.band(10,20)     ​6​
==>15
mtron> -15.band(-20,-10)     ​7​
mtron> band -> |inst?int{?}<=int(min=>?nat::T.else(-100),max=>?nat::T.else(100)){ is(and(gte(*min),lte(*max))) }
mtron> 15.band(10,20)     ​8​
==>15
mtron> -15.band(-20,-10)     ​9​
==>-15
    1 A nat is an int greater than zero.
    2 Argument name uniqueness does not consider query parameters.
    3 The ?nat query parameter requires the respective argument be some \(n \in \mathbb{N}\).
    anonymous inst
    \[\tt{obj}_\tt{lhs} \cdot \color{yellow}{\lambda}(a_0 \Rightarrow x, a_1 \Rightarrow y,\ldots,a_n) \mapsto \tt{obj}_\tt{rhs}\]

    An untyped inst is an anonymous inst and behaves analogously to an anonymous function in other programming environments. Anonymous insts can be both defined and evaluated in the same expression to provide immediately invoked function expression semantics. Like any inst, anonymous inst arguments are scoped to the value of the inst, providing block-scope semantics. Finally, a degenerate anonymous inst with no specified variable frame is simply bcode.

    mtron>  10.(min=>2,max=>8){ is(and(gte(*min),lte(*max))) }     ​1​
mtron>  10.(min=>minus(1),max=>plus(1)){ is(and(gte(*min),lte(*max))) }     ​2​
==>10
mtron>  10.(min=>2,max=>8){ is(and(gte(*bad_min),lte(*bad_max))) }     ​3​
mtron>  10.(){ is(and(gte(*min),lte(*max))) }     ​4​
mtron> 10.is(and(gte(minus(1)),lte(plus(1))))     ​5​
==>10
    1 The unnamed "band" inst is evaluated in line.
    2 min and max are computed dynamically relative to the input.
    3 The inst value is referencing undefined frame variables.
    4 An anonymous inst is compiled to an obj filter-inst.
    5 When arguments are embedded in the value, then the inst is simply bcode.
    contextual args
    dependent args
    \[\tt{obj}_\tt{in} \cdot f(a_0 \Rightarrow x,a_1 \Rightarrow \color{yellow}{g(\ast a_1)},\ldots,a_n) \mapsto \tt{obj}_\tt{out}\]

    If an argument’s type is dependent on the value of another argument, then the argument is a dependent argument.

    mtron> band -> |band?int{?}<=int(min=>_,max=>?>*min.else(*min+1)){ is(gte(*min)).is(lte(*max)) }
mtron> 2.band(min=>1,max=>1)     ​1​
    1 …​
    refined args
    mtron> is_divisible -> |is_divisble?bool<=int(by=>is(neq(0)).else(print('error').map(1))) { mod(*by).eq(0) }
mtron>  10.is_divisible(2)
==>true
mtron>  10.is_divisible('abc')
==>fail::[apply failure:
   	[lhs]    │ 10
   	 \_type  │ /m/int
   	  \_pred │ []
   	[inst]   │ mod?rng=int&dom=int('abc'){<j>}@(1)
   	 \_dom   │ int::T
   	 \_args  │ ['abc'][MTronException<137>:'abc' [value] unable to convert str::T]]['abc' [value] unable to convert str::T]@/sys/fail/208

    dom/rng coefficients

    Every inst has a dom and rng encoded in their key/value query parameters. The dom/rng query parameter values are any fURI minus query parameters (ie. recursive ? is not allowed). What is possible is the specification of a type structure, a coefficient, and polymorphic generics.

       /ex/inst/xyz?poly{c1,c2}[p1,p2,..,pn]<=mono{c3}
        ▲        ▲     ▲        ▲         ▲     ▲
        │        │     │        │         │     │
        │       tid  coeff    poly       tid  coeff
        │        └─────┬────────┘         └──┬──┘
     inst tid         rng                   dom

    An inst to create int triples can be written:

    mtron> trpl -> |inst?lst<=int{3}(){>-[,]}
mtron> {1,2,3,4,5,6}.trpl()
==>[1,2,3]
==>[4,5,6]

    The | token is syntactic sugar for the block inst.

    block?A<=#{?}(A::T)

    A blocked instruction prevents the application of the lhs to the inst args and inst code. This ensures the uri trpl is not applied to the inst prior to the uri being bound as the reference (→). Without block, the result of applying trpl to inst would be what trpl is bound to. In this particular instance, trpl would reference a fail obj.

    mtron>  trpl -> inst?lst[int,int,int]<=int{3}(){>-[,]}     ​1​
==>fail::[lhs range does not match inst domain: uri::T => int{3}::T [inst?rng=lst[int,int,int]&dom=int{3}(){merge([,])}]]@/sys/fail/210
mtron> trpl -> |inst?lst[int,int,int]<=int{3}(){>-[,]}     ​2​
mtron> *trpl     ​3​
    1 if the inst specification is not blocked, then trple is the failure.
    2 the inst specification blocked, enabling trple to be bound to the spec.
    3 dereferencing trple yields the inst specification.

    A stricter, more specific expression of trpl tha leverages polymorphic type constructions is as follows:

    mtron> trpl -> |inst?lst[int,int,int]<=int{3}(){>-[,]}
mtron> {1,2,3,4,5,6}.trpl()
==>[1,2,3]
==>[4,5,6]

    The trpl instruction code is composed of a single instruction: >-[,]. This syntactic sugar resolves merge([,]). The instruction signature of merge is A<=#{*}(A::T). This states that merge is a barrier instruction that maps the entire lhs stream of objs (#{*}) to a single A, where A is a generic bound to lst given the resolution of the argument A::T. Thus, this instance of merge yields a single lst whose elements are the three (int{3}) incoming lhs objs.

    Control Structures

    ┌────────────────────────────────┐
│data  o.   o...o  ..oo    o     │
│     o . :  o ▲ o    :  ..  o   │
│         o    │       o..   o   │
└──────────────┼─────────────────┘
               @
               └────┐
┌───────────────────┼────────────┐
│       ▲──────┐    ▼─────►    ▲ │
│  ─────┘      ▼────┘     │    │ │
│instructions             └────┘ │
└────────────────────────────────┘

    A fundamental requirement for general-purpose computing is branching. Branching occurs when a process chooses one of the myriad of paths through a body of code. This path is the process trace and in classical, non-quantum computing this trace is the result of numerous choices made by the process altering its state given its current instruction and data. Common branching idioms include if/else or while/do or switch/case. These structures typically reside outside the referential purview of the executing process and serve as an existential scaffolding that guides the unwitting process through the code and over the data — instruction by instruction and pointer by pointer.

    mtron has no language construct for branching. Instead, branching is realized as an epiphenomena of the evaluation of two instructions: split and merge (enabled through their respective coefficients).

    \[\begin{align*} \tt{split}&\tt{?}A\{*\} \Leftarrow A & \\ \tt{merge}&\tt{?}A\{*\} \Leftarrow A\{*\} & \end{align*}\]

    Individually, split and merge are "proto"-instructions in that, algebraically, they are not a complete computational step in and of themselves, but when combined, the two yield the abelian group of the mtron inst ring — i.e. the addition operator. When split, but not yet merged, the process is in a state akin to a quantum superposition. In fact, when coefficients are complex numbers, then constructive and destructive interference occurs, effectively rendering the mathematics of Schrödinger’s wave function equation, which for the computationally inclined, is perhaps best conceptualized by Feynman’s path integrals.

    \[\begin{align*} \tt{apply}&\tt{?} B\{\tt{*}\}\Leftarrow A \{\tt{*}\} \end{align*}\]

    The additive component of the mtron ring, split/merge (sugar’d -</>-), is distributive over the ring’s multiplicative monoid, apply (sugar’d .). The relationship between split/merge and apply is significant, with mtron coefficients serving as the glue which renders this coupling algebraically sound (closed under the ring operators). A complete analysis of this algebra is discussed elsewhere. For now, when seen in isolation as a commutative group, the following axioms hold.

    \[ \small \begin{aligned} &\tt{sm}(ca,noobj) &=&\; \tt{ca} + \tt{noobj} &=&\; \tt{ca} & (\text{zero identity})\\ &\tt{sm}(ca,cb) &=&\; \tt{ca} + \tt{cb} &=&\; \tt{sm}(cb,ca) & (\text{commutative})\\ &\tt{sm}(ca,{\text{-}ca}) &=&\; \tt{ca} - \tt{ca} &=&\; \tt{noobj} & (\text{inverse})\\ &\tt{sm}(ca,sm(cb,cd)) &=&\; \tt{ca} + (\tt{cb} + \tt{cd}) &=&\; \tt{sm(sm(ca,cb),cd)} & (\text{associative}).\\ \end{aligned} \]

    When a poly is an argument to split, a new poly is constructed as detailed below for both rec

    \[ \small \tt{obj} \cdot \tt{split}(\tt{rec}) \equiv \left[ \begin{aligned} \tt{obj} \cdot& \tt{dom({rec}_{0})} & \Rightarrow &\; \tt{obj} \cdot \tt{rng({rec}_{0})} \\ \tt{obj} \cdot& \tt{dom({rec}_{1})} & \Rightarrow &\; \tt{obj} \cdot \tt{rng({rec}_{1})} \\ \tt{obj} \cdot& \tt{dom({rec}_{2})} & \Rightarrow &\; \tt{obj} \cdot \tt{rng({rec}_{2})} \\ \vdots & & \Rightarrow &\; \vdots \\ \tt{obj} \cdot& \tt{dom({rec}_{n})} & \Rightarrow &\; \tt{obj} \cdot \tt{rng({rec}_{n})} \end{aligned} \right] \]

    and lst

    \[ \small \tt{obj} \cdot \tt{split}(\tt{lst}) \equiv \left[ \begin{aligned} \tt{obj} \cdot & \tt{dom({lst}_{0})} \\ \tt{obj} \cdot & \tt{rng({lst}_{1})} \\ \vdots &\\ \tt{obj} \cdot & \tt{rng({lst}_{n})} \\ \end{aligned} \right]. \]

    These constructions appear at odds with what is required for branching — namely, discrimination. The ability to choose one branch of code over another is entirely absent in the semantics of split. Every relation in a rec argument and every element in lst argument has the lhs obj applied to it — indiscriminately. Selective discrimination is a consequence of the properties of the abelian monoid (commutative/associative) and its zero element (noobj). Given that a rec relation can not have a noobj domain nor range, then selective branching is achieved programmatically through filtering. That is, given rec relations,

    mtron> [a=>1,b=>noobj,noobj=>3]
==>[a=>1]

    then,

    mtron> {1,2}     ​1​
==>1
==>2
mtron> {1,2}.split([is(lt(2))  => plus(9),
                    is(gte(2)) => plus(3)])
==>[1=>10]
==>[2=>5]
mtron> {1,2}.split([is(lt(2))  => plus(9),
                    is(gte(2)) => plus(3)]).rshift()     ​2​
==>10
==>5
    1 two int objs in a stream.
    2 each int obj is split (copied) across the branch arguments (the rels of the rec arg).
    3 gte?bool<=int(int::T) is a one-to-one function which sets up a binary switch (the process is in "superposition").
    4 is?int{?}<=int(bool::T) is a one-to-maybe function that filters paths given a binary signal (the process has chosen its path).
    5 merge?int<=int{*} collapses the two stream pathways into a single stream (the process has "collapsed" into a single branch).

    The above written once more using sugar syntax:

    mtron> {1,2}-<[?<2=>+9,?>=2=>+3]
==>[1=>10]
==>[2=>5]
mtron> {1,2}-<[?<2=>+9,?>=2=>+3]>>
==>10
==>5

    • -< split.

    • ?< is less than.

    • ?> is greater than.

    • >- merge.

    Unlike rec, lst can have noobj elements. However, when merged into a single stream, noobj are discarded.

    mtron> {1,2}-<[gte(2),gt(3)]     ​1​
==>[false,false]
==>[true,false]
mtron> {1,2}-<[?>=2,?>3]     ​2​
==>[noobj,noobj]
==>[2,noobj]
mtron> {1,2}-<[?>=2,?>3]>-     ​3​
==>2
    1 Each incoming obj is split across each "branch" (position) of the lst.
    2 a lst can have noobj elements (empty positions).
    3 The lst is merged into a single stream, thus, filtering out noobj branches.

    While gte?bool<=int(int::T) yields a bool, is?int{?}<=int(bool::T) yields the requisite switch behavior in that the lhs obj is either allowed to pass or not depending on the bool argument. Given inst evaluation semantics, the value of the bool::T argument of is is ultimately dependent on the incoming lhs obj, where

    \[\tt{obj} \cdot \tt{gte}(\_ ) \cdot \tt{is}(\_ ) => obj\{?\} \in \{\tt{obj},\tt{noobj}\}.\]

    In this way, the mechanics of inst lhs-resolution, noobj as the 0-element of the obj ring, and it’s dual usage in both * (filter) and + (clone) provide sufficient infrastructure to render branch semantics in mtron.

    if/else

    mtron> {1,2,3,4,5}-<[?>2 ⇒ "${} greater than 2", ?>5 ⇒ "${} greater than 5", _ ⇒ "${_} is something."] =⇒[1⇒'1 is something.'] =⇒[2⇒'2 is something.'] =⇒[3⇒{'3 greater than 2','3 is something.'}] =⇒[4⇒{'4 greater than 2','4 is something.'}] =⇒[5⇒{'5 greater than 2','5 is something.'}]

    documentation setup 
    mtron>  import(/m/web)
...
mtron>  import(/m/llm)
...
mtron>  import(/m/math)
...
mtron> httpspace::[host     => http://localhost:8777,
                   q        => [mimeq::[=>]],
                   pattern  => <http://#>,
                   route    => [/mcp     => mcp_mtron_http,
                                /       => <>]]@/sys/space/web/http
mtron> wsspace::[host     => ws://localhost:8555,
                 pattern  => <ws://#>,
                 q        => [docq::[=>],subq::[=>]],
                 route    => [/mtron  => mtron_ws,
                              /mcp    => mcp_mtron_ws]]@/sys/space/web/ws
mtron> fsspace::[pattern  => fs:#,
                 route    => [fs:=><.metatron>]]@/sys/space/fs

    Modern AI techniques are heavily leveraged across various aspects of metatron, with popular cloud service providers (e.g. Anthropic and/or OpenAI) and local providers (e.g. Ollama, LMStudio, and/or LocalAI) serving as the compute-intensive inferencing backends.

    1. Pair Programmer: LLMs can connect to a console session, be provided a terminal split screen, and evaluate mtron expressions alongside the user. This is especially useful when developing strategies for interlinking spaces via schema alignment and entity resolution as well as quickly creating domain-specific rewrite rules to optimize mtron compilation (using mtron).

    2. Agent Development: A large portion of an LLM’s functional elements are embedded in the URI address space and made accessible to mtron via respective spaces. These components are called features and metatron offers a rich set of features that can can be composed to yield custom agent behaviors:

      • tools: every mtron inst has an as()-mapping to tool, where the docq processor of the inst provides the name, description, and argument notes necessary for LLM tool selection. While most mtron instructions are low-level and not necessarily "tool-oriented," user-defined custom instructions can be defined and offered as tools/services to llms as needed.

      • thoughts: when enabling internal reasoning with a supporting LLM, the stream of thought is published streamed and can be directed to system output, to a memory structure, to another LLM for review, etc.

      • sessions: an LLMs interactions and thoughts can be stored and retrieved at a later time to enable long-term memory and context awareness.

      • skills: an LLM can be trained on a wide range of skills and domains, allowing them to perform complex tasks and solve domain-specific problems. Skills can be pulled from the Anthropic-specified .agent directory that has a corresponding SKILL.md file and associated resources. Or, can be created programmatically in metaron.

      • notes: pass information to an LLM during a chat iteration without interrupting them. LLMs will periodically check their note stack for any advice/information from the user about their task at hand.

      • prompts: specific prompts or instructions can be stored and retrieved to help guide an LLM’s response.

      • response formats: a mtron rec can serve as a schema object instructing the LLM to populate the rec with their answer to avoid arbitrary string responses in favor of structured responses.

      • concepts: generative memory structures that extract ideas from agent chat histories in order to provide semantic indices into the agents verbatim memory structures.

      • gguff vector files: a training feature…​

    3. MCP Servers: Developing and deploying MCP servers in mtron as well as interacting with remote MCP servers as a client is straightforward. MCP servers and their tools offer numerous ways by which LLMs can interact with their environment.

    Models

    A model::T references a LLM endpoint.

    mtron> */m/llm/model

    Models can be either cloud-based AI LLMs or local LLMs. Two example models are provided below.

    mtron> model::[provider => ollama,
               protocol => ollama,
               host     => http://localhost:11434,
               llm      => <qwen3.5:4b>]
==>model::[
    skill=>[completion,vision,tools,thinking],
    size=>gB::3.1572,
    context=>262144,
    provider=>ollama,
    protocol=>ollama,
    host=>http://localhost:11434,
    llm=><qwen3.5:4b>]
mtron> model::[host     => <https://api.deepseek.com/v1>,
               api_key  => !*</sys/env/DEEPSEEK_API_KEY>,
               provider => deepseek,
               protocol => openai,
               llm      => <deepseek-v4-pro>]
==>model::[
    name=>deepseek-v4-pro,
    host=><https://api.deepseek.com/v1>,
    api_key=>!*/sys/env/DEEPSEEK_API_KEY,
    provider=>deepseek,
    protocol=>openai,
    llm=>deepseek-v4-pro]

    The majority of cloud and local LLM providers expose HTTP/REST endpoints. These endpoints can be programmatically inspected using mtron in order to locate a model that meets a particular use case requirement. For example, the expression belows is used to find a suitable model for the examples in this documentation. This expression makes two Ollama API calls:

    • get all models that are larger than 1 gig and smaller than 5 gigs.

    • filter down to those models that have thinking and tool capabilities.

    The resultant Ollama rec::T is then morphed into a model::T.

    There are two expressions in the example below that are bound by an intermediate ollama_model stack reference. The purpose of breaking up the example into two expression is to highlight the Ollama representation of a model before morphing it into a model::T ready for use in the /m/llm instruction set. 
    mtron> *http://localhost:11434/api/tags/+.>>.
         =?=[size=>as(real::T).as(bB::T).
                   ?>gB::1.0.
                   ?<gB::5.0].
         -<[_=>-<[name=>>>name.as(str::T)].
               to(http://localhost:11434/api/show).
               =?=[capabilities=>has(thinking).has(tools)]].dom().
         order(>>size * -1).
         take(1).to(ollama_model)
    1 stream all the models currently available on the local ollama server.
    2 filter out models too small (for ollama, the cloud models) and models too big.
    3 create rec::T with domain the ollama model and range further details about the model.
    4 an http POST is made and only those details that have thinking and tools capabilites remain.
    5 the domain of the intermediate rec::T is projected out.
    6 reverse sort the ollama models by their size.
    7 select the smallest ollama model of those remaining.
    8 store the result in an intermediate stack variable called ollama_model. 
    mtron> *ollama_model.>>name.
         -<[host     => http://localhost:11434,
            provider => ollama,
            protocol => ollama,
            llm      => as(uri::T)].as(model::T).
         to(metatron_model)

    a note on unit conversions

    The size relation maps to a bB::T (bytes) while the where() clause filtered on gB::T (gigabytes). The conversion between the different data size units is handled automatically by the type constructors of those data size types. These types are distributed in the /m/math instruction set and imported via import(/m/math) (or import(/m/math,math:) if naming conflicts may emerge).

    mtron> */m/math.>>type.>>     ​1​
==>int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat
==>real::T[id()]@/m/math/data
==>data::T[][ctor?bB<=#{?}(#{*}::T)]@/m/math/data/bB
==>data::T[][ctor?kB<=#{?}(#{*}::T)]@/m/math/data/kB
==>data::T[][ctor?mB<=#{?}(#{*}::T)]@/m/math/data/mB
==>data::T[][ctor?gB<=#{?}(#{*}::T)]@/m/math/data/gB
==>data::T[][ctor?tB<=#{?}(#{*}::T)]@/m/math/data/tB
==>data::T[][ctor?pB<=#{?}(#{*}::T)]@/m/math/data/pB
==>real::T[?real::T]@/m/math/currency
==>currency::T@/m/math/currency/usd
==>currency::T@/m/math/currency/euro
==>real::T[?real::T]@/m/math/time
==>time::T[][ctor?millis<=#{?}(#{*}::T)]@/m/math/time/millis
==>time::T[][ctor?second<=#{?}(#{*}::T)]@/m/math/time/second
==>time::T[][ctor?minute<=#{?}(#{*}::T)]@/m/math/time/minute
==>time::T[][ctor?hour<=#{?}(#{*}::T)]@/m/math/time/hour
==>time::T[][ctor?day<=#{?}(#{*}::T)]@/m/math/time/day
==>uri::T[/m/inst/pred?rng=#{?}&dom=#{?}(#{*}::T){<j>}]@/m/math/datetime
    1 get the types defined in /m/math instset and then drain that lst to a stream to see a more detailed string representation. 
    mtron> gB::500.0.as(mB::T)     ​1​
==>mB::512000.0000
mtron> gB::500.0.as(kB::T)     ​2​
==>kB::524288000.0000
mtron> tB::1.0.as(mB::T)     ​3​
==>mB::1048576.0000
    1 convert gigabytes to megabytes
    2 convert gigabytes to kilobytes
    3 convert terabytes to megabytes

    Agents

    The most important type in /m/llm is agent::T. While a model::T represents the gateway to a particular data structure and inferencing engine, an agent::T subsumes one or more model::T in its functioning. Along with models, and agent can be equipped with other data structures and processes that provide a richer, more intelligent entity within metatron. These supplemental structures and processes are collectively known as features. The metatron encoding of an example agent distributed with metatron named Dr.Stynx is provided below. Over the remainder of this documentation, each of the components of Dr.Stynx’s representation will be made clear.

    agent::[
  name     => 'dr.stynx',
  desc     => 'the agent dr.stynx is here to be of use in the metatron',
  root     => !*boot/args/internal/agent_home,
  feature  => [
    message_feature::::[on_agent_ctor => inst(a=>agent::T){ *</usr/dr/session/1>.else(
                                           session::[agent     => !*boot/args/internal/agent_home,
                                                     user      => !*boot/args/internal/user_home,
                                                     name      => 'general chat',
                                                     algorithm => [name=><message_window>, max=>20]]
                                                           ==[_=>_].to(/usr/dr/session/1)) },
                        session       => /usr/dr/session/1],
    chat_feature:::::::[model         => !(*</usr/dr/model>.else(model::[provider     => ollama,
                                                                         protocol     => ollama,
                                                                         host         => <http://ginger.local:11434>,
                                                                         llm          => <qwen3.8:27b>])),
                        response      => [complete => -<modal::[title => '{{r}}agent response{{b}}',
                                                                body  => "{{y}}${>>chat}",
                                                                style => style::[border    =>continuous,
                                                                                 background=>"{{[k]}}",
                                                                                 foreground=>"{{b}}",
                                                                                 zIndex    => 100]].display()],
                        root          => /usr/dr/chat_result],
    think_feature::::::[on_agent_ctor => inst(a=>agent::T){
                                            accordion::[title => "thoughts", 
                                                        body  => "",
                                                        style => style::[anchor=>top_left,left=>1,top=>3,height=>10,width=>80]]@<think_widget> },
                       on_before_chat => inst(){ @<think_widget> >>= [body=>""] },
                       root           => /usr/dr/message,
                       think          => [to => inst(a=>str::T){ @<think_widget>>>=[body=>+*a].display() }]],
    compaction_feature::[model        => !*/usr/dr/model,
                         threshold    => 0.8],
    loop_feature:::::::[max_loop      => 100,
                        root          => /usr/dr/loop],
    comment_feature::::[              => ],
    skill_feature::::::[skill         => [ <mfs:.metatron/skills/metatron>, 
                                           <mfs:.metatron/skills/mtron>,
                                           <mfs:.metatron/skills/ide>]],
    concept_feature::::[root          => /usr/dr/concept,
                        extractor     => lucene],
    audit_feature::::::[on_agent_ctor => inst(a=>agent::T) {   
                                           accordion::[title  => "audit",
                                                       body   => "",
                                                       style  => style::[anchor=>bottom_right,width=>65,height=>30,
                                                                         left=>-2,top=>8,foreground =>"{{y}}"]]@<audit_widget> },
                        to            => inst(a=>str::T){ @<audit_widget>>>=[body=>+*a].display() },
                        root          => /usr/dr/audit],
    bash_feature:::::::[reject        => ["\brm\b"]],
    summarize_feature::[root          => /usr/dr],
    embed_feature::::::[root          => embed:chat_result,
                        model         => model::[provider     => ollama,
                                                 protocol     => ollama,
                                                 host         => <http://ginger.local:11434>,
                                                 llm          => <qwen3-embedding:4b>]],
    system_feature:::::[              => ],
    tool_feature:::::::[tool          => [!*eval,!*sql,!*codegraph,
                                          forget?noobj{0}<=noobj{0}(){ /usr/dr/message/+ -> noobj }]],
    cost_feature:::::::[root          => /usr/dr/cost,
                        to            => inst(in_cost=>real::T,out_cost=>real::T,total_cost=>real::T){ 
                                           /usr/marko/console/status("cost total: ${*2} [in: ${*0} out: ${*1}]") },
                        rate          => [in  => usd_currency::0.435,
                                          out => usd_currency::0.870]]]]@<dr>;

    Features

    The following subsections focus on a particular /m/llm feature.

    Instruction Sets

    \[\texttt{inst} \in (\underbrace{\texttt{obj}}_{\text{the lhs object}} \times \underbrace{\texttt{inst}}_{\text{the instruction}}) \rightarrow \underbrace{\texttt{obj}}_{\text{the rhs object}}\]

    An instruction set is defined as a record (rec) of the form:

    mtron> *instset
    \[\begin{align*} \texttt{inst} \in (\underbrace{\texttt{obj}}_{\text{the lhs object}} \times \underbrace{\texttt{inst}}_{\text{the instruction}}) \rightarrow \underbrace{\texttt{obj}}_{\text{the rhs object}} \end{align*}\]

    Fail and Catch

    • fail space
    • the cause of failure

    The exception handling architecture of metatron leverages a fail monad with some unique features novel to metatron. The fail monad has two states: propagating and caught.

    fail state fail state description fail state diagram

    propagating

    plus?<=int(uri::T) is not a valid instruction and a fail::T monad is created.

    [HIDDEN] */sys/fail/+.catch(_)
1.plus(b).plus(3).mult(5)
    		 
          ┌─────────────────►
      │      (fail space)
──────►
1.plus(b).plus(3).mult(5)

    caught

    catch?A{*}<=A(A{*}::T) passes all objs from lhs to rhs except for a fail::T. A lhs fail::T is applied to the catch() args the result of the catch() body is emitted rhs.

    mtron> 1.plus(b).plus(3).catch(2).mult(5)
==>10
    		 
          ┌───────────┐
      │           │   (inst space)
──────►           ▼──────────────►
1.plus(b).plus(3).catch(2).mult(5)

    fail monad

    The diagram above illustrates the concept of monadic computing, where when an exception occurs, the obj is immediately "lifted" out of the instruction space (the graph of inst objs composing a code::T) and continues to propagate to the right unaffected by any subsequent instructions until a "flattening" instruction is met — namely catch(). At which point, catch() tags the fail::T caught and bring it back down into the computational flow of the instruction space.

    fail space

    The diagram above demonstrates that uncaught fail::T objs are routed to fail space. When an exception occurs and a fail monad is created, the fail::T obj is immediately written to the subspace identified at boot/fail_pattern. The uri /sys/fail?incr=./+ is the default fail space pattern. There are two reasons for this behavior.

    1. The exception hasn’t been handled and can be resumed at a later time in a coroutine-esque manner. This is possible even after the underlying machine has completed execution of the instruction graph.

    2. The exception stack trace can be clipped to reduce visual clutter, and only when accessed and caught from fail space will the fail’s trace be unrolled and available to introspection.

    mtron>  1.plus(b)     ​1​
==>fail::[apply failure:
   	[lhs]    │ 1
   	 \_type  │ /m/int
   	  \_pred │ []
   	[inst]   │ plus?rng=uri{?}&dom=int(b){<j>}
   	 \_dom   │ int::T
   	 \_args  │ [b][MTronException<137>:1 [int::T] unable to convert uri::T]][1 [int::T] unable to convert uri::T]@/sys/fail/60
mtron> */sys/fail/+     ​2​
mtron> */sys/fail/+.catch(_)     ​3​
mtron> */sys/fail     ​4​
    1 A fail::T monad is generated and is not caught.
    2 The uncaught fail::T is written to /sys/fail using the incrq query processor.
    3 The uncaught fail::T remains uncaught until caught.
    4 A caught fail::T is removed from space.

    */sys/fail/+.catch(_) is useful code for clearing the fail space and releasing memory.

    the cause of failure

    Finally, the cause() instruction enables recursive stack walking for reflective exception handling.

    mtron> *cause     ​1​
mtron> 1+b.catch(cause())     ​2​
==>fail::[1 [int::T] unable to convert uri::T]
mtron> throw('bad').catch(cause())     ​3​
    1 The cause() instruction has a domain of fail::T and range of fail{?}::T. The range being potentially a previous underlying fail::T to the current/domain fail::T.
    2 Once a fail::T is caught, it can be applied to cause() for introspection.
    3 throw() can be used to create a fail::T monad.

    A Hybrid Structural/Nominal Type System

    • Sturctural Types
    • Type Predicates
    • Structural Type Hierarchy
    • Type Constructors
    • cascading type constructions
    • mono types
    • bytes
    • poly types
    • call types
    • instructions
    • variables
    • mtron core types
    • instruction set architecture types
    documentation setup 
    mtron> import(/m/math)
mtron> int::T[?>0]@nat

    Every obj in mtron has a type. A type is either a base type or a refinement on a base type. For instance, int::5 is a value with type int::T (a base type). An example refinement of int::T is the type int::T[?<10]. The refinement type states that a value of this type is any int::T less than 10. A similar, though more consequential example is the set of natural numbers \(\mathbb{N}\) being a subset of the set of integers \(\mathbb{Z}\). That is, \(\mathbb{N} \subset \mathbb{Z}\). The set of natural numbers is defined in mtron as:

    a structural type
              int::T[?>0]@nat
          │    │ │    │
          │    │ │    └──────────── type vid (type being defined)
          │    │ └───────────────── predicate inst pred?int{?}<=int(int::T)
          │    └─────────────────── type token
          └──────────────────────── type tid (type being refined)

    Non-base types will be notated B::T < A::T: " B::T captures a subset of the values captured by A::T." When notated B::T == A::T: " B::T has the same values as A::T ". When two types have the same values but different names (vids) There exists only a nominal difference between A::T and B::T. In such situation, values of A::T and values of B::T are different in that they are being type/classified as being distinct through label (through name). As an example, the type int::T@age is not the same as int::T@zipcode. The structure of a nominal type is specified as:

    a nominal type
              int::T@nat
          │    |  │
          │    │  └──────────────── type vid (type being named)
          │    └─────────────────── type token
          └──────────────────────── type tid (type being renamed)
    The distinction between structural and nominal types depends solely on the existence of a predicate which specifies the structural requirements a value must satisfy to be considered that type. With no constraint on structure, then the implied constraint is on vid, i.e. name.

    Sturctural Types

    Type Predicates

    A predicate is any inst of the form

    \[ \tt pred?A\{?\} \Leftarrow A()\{ code \}, \]

    such that a lhs value of type A is mapped by code to a rhs value of either type A::T (true) or A{0}::T (false). Hence, A{?} which is sugar for A{0,1}.

    A structural type is any type defined with a predicate. 
    mtron> int::4     ​1​
==>4
mtron> nat::4     ​2​
==>nat::4
mtron> int::-4     ​3​
==>-4
mtron>  nat::-4     ​4​
==>fail::[-4 is not a int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat]@/sys/fail/560
mtron> rec::T[?[name=>str::T,age=>int::T]]@chicken     ​5​
mtron> chicken::[name=>'snowbutt',age=>7]     ​6​
==>chicken::[name=>'snowbutt',age=>7]
mtron>  chicken::[name=>'punky',age=>'four']     ​7​
==>fail::[obj does not match chicken::T
     [name=>'punky',age=>'four']
         X=>
     animal::T[?[name=>str::T,age=>int::T]]@chicken
     ----------------------------------------------
     [
      name=>(==>name)=>'punky'=>==>str::T,
      age=>(/=>age)=>'four'=>X=>int::T]]@/sys/fail/562
    1 The value 4 is an integer.
    2 The value 4 is a natural number.
    3 The value -4 is an integer.
    4 The value -4 is not a natural number.
    5 A chicken is defined as a rec::T with a name and an age.
    6 Snowbutt is a 7 year old chicken.
    7 Punky’s age is an string, not an integer and thus, not a chicken by definition.

    Structural Type Hierarchy

    A structural type can be refined with further structural requirements. An example is the concept of age. No human being in modern recorded history has been reported to be over the age of 120 years old. Furthermore, no person has a negative age as at birth, the age is 0. Together, age can be defined as any natural number less than 120 (0 > age < 120).

    The examples below make use of mtron’s as?B<=A(B::T) instruction. For the purpose of these examples, as() is being used to determine whether a value meets the structural requirements of the respective type. The as() instruction also handles type constructors, and this aspect of a type is discussed later. 
    mtron> int::T[?>0]@nat     ​1​
mtron> nat::T[?<120]@age     ​2​
mtron> 21.as(int::T).as(nat::T).as(age::T)     ​3​
==>age::21
mtron>  -21.as(int::T).as(nat::T).as(age::T)     ​4​
==>fail::[apply failure:
   	[lhs]    │ -21
   	 \_type  │ /m/int
   	  \_pred │ []
   	[inst]   │ as?rng=nat&dom=int(nat::T){<j>}@(2)
   	 \_dom   │ int::T
   	 \_args  │ [nat::T][MTronException<137>:-21 is not a int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat [structural]]][-21 is not a int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat [structural]]@/sys/fail/564
mtron>  121.as(int::T).as(nat::T).as(age::T)     ​5​
==>fail::[apply failure:
   	[lhs]    │ nat::121
   	 \_type  │ /m/math/nat
   	  \_pred │ [is(gt(0))]
   	[inst]   │ as?rng=age&dom=int(age::T){<j>}@(3)
   	 \_dom   │ int::T
   	 \_args  │ [age::T][MTronException<137>:nat::121 is not a nat::T[is(lt(120))]@age [structural]]][nat::121 is not a nat::T[is(lt(120))]@age [structural]]@/sys/fail/566
mtron> rec::T[?[name=>str::T,age=>age::T]]@chicken     ​6​
mtron> chicken::[name=>'snowbutt',age=>7]     ​7​
==>chicken::[name=>'snowbutt',age=>7]
mtron>  chicken::[name=>'punky',age=>444]     ​8​
==>fail::[obj does not match chicken::T
     [name=>'punky',age=>444]
         X=>
     animal::T[?[
       name=>str::T,
       age=>age::T]]@chicken
     -------------------------------------
     [
      name=>(==>name)=>'punky'=>==>str::T,
      age=>(/=>age)=>444=>X=>age::T,
      age=>(==>age)=>444=>==>int::T]]@/sys/fail/568
    1 A natural number is any integer greater than 0.
    2 An age is any natural number less than 120.
    3 21 is an int, nat, and age.
    4 -21 is an int, but not a nat and thus, not an age.
    5 121 is an int, nat, but not an age.
    6 A chicken’s age has been redefined to be an age::T.
    7 Snowbutt is still, according to the new definition, a chicken.
    8 Punky’s specified age places him outside the definition of a chicken.

    Nominal Types

    As stated in at the beginning of this document, mtron supports both structural and nominal type specifications. A nominal type is any type with no specified predicate. This means that all the base types (e.g. int::T, bool::T, rec::T, etc.) are nominal types. A base type is any type whose tid is equal to its vid. For example, and analogously for the other base types, int::T is defined as:

    mtron> int::T@int

    From mtron’s vantage point at it’s level of abstraction in computing, there is nothing in the definition of a base type that distinguishes it from another base type save the vid/name.

    An int is an int because it is named int. 
    mtron> str::T@re     ​1​
mtron> *regex     ​2​
mtron> 'marko'.regex('m.{3}o')     ​3​
==>['marko']
mtron> 'marko'.regex(re::'m.{3}o')     ​4​
==>['marko']
    1 re::T is a regular expression string.
    2 regex is an instruction that takes a re::T as an argument.
    3 a str::T is not an re::T nominally.
    4 regex with with a particular type of nominally defined string.

    Integrating Nominal Types with Structural Types

    With structural types, any rec::T can be a chicken::T as long as it meets the structural requirements of chicken::T. This means, that if a rec::T denotes (in the mind of the engineer) a human (who has a name and age), that human is also a chicken. This can be problematic. For those situations when the value has no other discriminating qualities for a predicate to leverage, then an assigned type label becomes a useful taxonomical criteria.

    mtron> rec::T[?[name=>str::T,age=>age::T]]@being     ​1​
mtron> being::T@human     ​2​
mtron> being::T@chicken     ​3​
mtron>  human::[name=>'marko',age=>29].as(chicken::T)     ​4​
==>chicken::[name=>'marko',age=>29]
mtron>  chicken::[name=>'snowbutt',age=>7].as(human::T)     ​5​
==>fail::[apply failure:
   	[lhs]    │ chicken::[name=>'snowbutt',age=>7]
   	 \_type  │ chicken
   	  \_pred │ [isa([
     name=>str::T,
     age=>age::T]), isa([name=>str::T,age=>int::T])]
   	[inst]   │ as?rng=human&dom=rec(human::T){<j>}@(1)
   	 \_dom   │ rec::T
   	 \_args  │ [human::T][MTronException<137>:chicken::[name=>'snowbutt',age=>7] is not a human::T [nominal]]][chicken::[name=>'snowbutt',age=>7] is not a human::T [nominal]]@/sys/fail/570
mtron> [name=>'fuzzy feet',age=>2].as(chicken::T)     ​6​
==>chicken::[name=>'fuzzy feet',age=>2]
mtron> 'punky'.as(chicken::T)     ​7​
==>fail::[apply failure:
   	[lhs]    │ 'punky'
   	 \_type  │ /m/str
   	  \_pred │ []
   	[inst]   │ as?rng=chicken&dom=str(chicken::T){<j>}@(1)
   	 \_dom   │ str::T
   	 \_args  │ [chicken::T][MTronException<137>:'punky' is not a animal::T[?[...]]['punky' is not a animal::T[?[
     name=>str::T,
     age=>age::T]]@chicken [structural]]@/sys/fail/572
    1 A being is a rec::T with a name and age.
    2 A person is a type of being.
    3 A chicken is a type of being.
    4 The person named marko is not a chicken.
    5 The chicken name snowbutt is not a person.
    6 A base type value can be nominalized…​
    7 …​as long as the base type of the nominal type matches.

    Structurally speaking, there is nothing that prevents the person named marko from also being a chicken. However, nominally, that is not the case. The person named marko has been nominally relegated to the taxonomical classification of a person. In this way, nominal and structural types co-exist in mtron in order to satisfy two different understandings of type (type by name and type by strufcture).

    Realize the distinction between structural and nominal types is one of convenience. It is true that there exists structural features of marko that make this person not a chicken (e.g. DNA, weight and size, etc.). However, if the system operating with such types can not make that distinction (perhaps no sensors to do so), then an explicit statement of the fact that marko is not a chicken allows such unrefined systems (literally) to operate effectively using said classification. This is the difference between experiential knowledge (structural) and authoratative knowledge (nominal).
    Type definitions in space are monotonic: once a type is defined with a predicate or constructor, subsequent redefinitions can only add structural constraints, never remove them. If person::T is written as a structural type and later referenced as being::T\@person (a nominal definition with no predicate), the existing predicate is inherited — the type remains structural. This prevents retroactive invalidation of values already constructed and checked against the original type contract. To define a fresh nominal type at a point in space that previously held a structural type, explicitly clear the type from space first (e.g., person -> noobj).

    Type Constructors

              nat::T[?>0][-<|[?<0 => * -1,_ => _]>>]
          │    │ │    │
          │    │ │    └──────────── constructor
          │    │ └───────────────── predicate
          │    └─────────────────── type symbol
          └──────────────────────── tid

    A quick explanation of the type predicate and constructor code is as follows:

    1. predicate [?>0]: is (?) greater than (>) 0.

    2. constructor [-<|[?<0 => ⋅-1,_ => _]>>]: if (-<|) less than (<) 0, then (=>) multiply (*) by -1, else do (=>) nothing (_). Yield the range (>>) of the relation constructed (the specifics of >> will be discussed later).

    Example usage of the defined nat type is presented below.

    mtron> nat -> int::T[?>0][-<|[is(lt(0)) => * -1,_ => _]>>]     ​1​
mtron> *nat     ​2​
mtron> nat::2     ​3​
==>nat::2
mtron> nat::-2     ​4​
==>fail::[-2 is not a int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat]@/sys/fail/574
mtron> 2.as(nat::T)     ​5​
==>nat::2
mtron>  -2.as(nat::T)     ​6​
==>fail::[apply failure:
   	[lhs]    │ -2
   	 \_type  │ /m/int
   	  \_pred │ []
   	[inst]   │ as?rng=nat&dom=int(nat::T){<j>}@(1)
   	 \_dom   │ int::T
   	 \_args  │ [nat::T][MTronException<137>:-2 is not a int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat [structural]]][-2 is not a int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat [structural]]@/sys/fail/576
mtron>  nat::2 * -1     ​7​
==>fail::[apply failure:
   	[lhs]    │ nat::2
   	 \_type  │ /m/math/nat
   	  \_pred │ [is(gt(0))]
   	[inst]   │ mult?rng=int&dom=int(-1){<j>}@(1)
   	 \_dom   │ int::T
   	 \_args  │ [-1][MTronException<137>:-2 is not a int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat]][-2 is not a int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat]@/sys/fail/578
    1 A natural number (nat) is a positive int. The predicate component of the type definition tests an int to determine if it’s a nat and the constructor component will generate a positive int given any int (i.e. the absolute value).
    2 the nat tid references the respective nat type obj.
    3 2 is an nat.
    4 -2 is not a nat, but given the constructor, becomes a nat.

    While every type is an obj, not every obj is a type. Those objs that are not types are instances of some type. This is the foundation for all other objs in {{c}}M{{g}}E{{y}}t{{g}}a{{r}}t{{m}}R{{g}}o{{m}}n{{X}}.

    cascading type constructions

    mono types

    An obj that is not composed of other objs is a mono type and are exhaustively itemized in the table below.

    type jvm object description

    bytes

    ByteBuffer

    …​

    bool

    Boolean

    …​

    int

    Long

    …​

    real

    Double

    …​

    str

    String

    …​

    uri

    fURI

    …​

    		 
    mtron> 0xabcd + 0x1234     ​1​
==>0xabcd1234
mtron> true + false     ​2​
==>true
mtron> 1 + 2     ​3​
==>3
mtron> 1.34 + 2.45     ​4​
==>3.7900
mtron> "abc" + "def"     ​5​
==>'abcdef'
mtron> a/b/c * d/e/f     ​6​
==>a/b/c/d/e/f

    The code below demonstrates the typical, expected uses of the mono types.

    bytes

    The bytes type represents binary data as a sequence of bytes.

    Examples:

    mtron> 'metatron'.as(bytes::T)     ​1​
==>0x6d65746174726f6e
mtron> bytes::'48656c6c6f'     ​2​
==>fail::['48656c6c6f' [string] is not a bytes::T]@/sys/fail/588
    1 Convert string to bytes
    2 Create bytes from hexadecimal representation

    Type Conversion with .as():

    Byte units can be converted between each other using the .as() instruction:

    mtron> kB::1024.0.as(mB::T)     ​1​
==>mB::1.0000
mtron> mB::1.0.as(kB::T)     ​2​
==>kB::1024.0000
mtron> gB::5.0.as(mB::T)     ​3​
==>mB::5120.0000
mtron> pB::1.0.as(tB::T)     ​4​
==>tB::1024.0000
    1 Convert 1024 kilobytes to megabytes (result: 1 mB)
    2 Convert 1 megabyte to kilobytes (result: 1024 kB)
    3 Convert 5 gigabytes to megabytes (result: 5120 mB)
    4 Convert 1 petabyte to terabytes (result: 1024 tB)

    Equality Comparison with .eq():

    Byte units of different types can be compared for equality:

    mtron> mB::1024.0.eq(gB::1.0)     ​1​
==>true
mtron> kB::1024.0.eq(mB::1.0)     ​2​
==>true
mtron> gB::2.0.eq(mB::2048.0)     ​3​
==>true
mtron> tB::1.0.eq(gB::1024.0)     ​4​
==>true
    1 Check if 1024 megabytes equals 1 gigabyte (result: true)
    2 Check if 1024 kilobytes equals 1 megabyte (result: true)
    3 Check if 2 gigabytes equals 2048 megabytes (result: true)
    4 Check if 1 terabyte equals 1024 gigabytes (result: true)

    Common Use Cases:

    • File size calculations: file_size.as(mB::T) to convert to megabytes

    • Storage capacity: disk_capacity.as(gB::T) to express in gigabytes

    • Data transfer rates: Compare bandwidth in different units

    • Memory allocation: Express memory sizes in appropriate units

    • Quota management: Set and compare storage quotas

    Binary vs Decimal Units:

    Metatron’s byte units follow the binary (base-2) convention where:

    • 1 kB = 1024 bytes (not 1000)

    • 1 mB = 1024 kB = 1,048,576 bytes (not 1,000,000)

    • 1 gB = 1024 mB = 1,073,741,824 bytes (not 1,000,000,000)

    This is consistent with how computer systems typically measure memory and storage internally.

    poly types

    Poly types are branched structures where each element or entry represents a separate branch. The two primary poly types are:

    lst (List) - Ordered collection where each element is a branch:

    mtron> [a,b,c]     ​1​
==>[a,b,c]
mtron> [1,2,3]     ​2​
==>[1,2,3]
mtron> [,]     ​3​
==>[,]
    1 List with three branches: a, b, and c
    2 List with three integer branches
    3 Empty list (no branches)

    rec (Record) - Key-value pairs where each entry is a branch:

    mtron> [a=>1,b=>2,c=>3]     ​1​
==>[a=>1,b=>2,c=>3]
mtron> [name=>'Alice',age=>30]     ​2​
==>[name=>'Alice',age=>30]
mtron> [=>]     ​3​
==>[=>]
    1 Record with three branches: a⇒1, b⇒2, and c⇒3
    2 Record representing a person
    3 Empty record (no branches)

    Key Characteristics:

    • Each element/entry is a separate branch

    • .count() returns 1 (the poly object itself)

    • >-.count() merges branches and counts elements/entries

    • Branches can be processed in parallel

    • Support pattern matching and wildcards

    Branching in Action:

    mtron> [1,2,3]     ​1​
==>[1,2,3]
mtron> [1,2,3].count()     ​2​
==>1
mtron> [1,2,3]>-.count()     ​3​
==>3
mtron> [a=>1,b=>2].count()     ​4​
==>1
mtron> [a=>1,b=>2]>-.count()     ​5​
==>2
    1 List with 3 branches
    2 Returns 1 (the list object itself)
    3 Returns 3 (merges branches, counts elements)
    4 Returns 1 (the record object itself)
    5 Returns 2 (merges branches, counts entries)

    Operations on Poly Types:

    mtron> [1,2,3].reverse()     ​1​
==>[3,2,1]
mtron> [1,2,3].plus([4,5])     ​2​
==>[1,2,3,4,5]
mtron> [1,2,3].merge()     ​3​
==>1
==>2
==>3
mtron> [a=>1,b=>2].dom()     ​4​
==>a
==>b
mtron> [a=>1,b=>2].rng()     ​5​
==>1
==>2
mtron> [a=>1,b=>2,c=>3].select([a=>_,b=>_])     ​6​
==>[a=>1,b=>2]
    1 Reverse list order
    2 Concatenate lists
    3 Merge list into a set
    4 Get domain (keys) of record
    5 Get range (values) of record
    6 Select specific keys from record

    call types

    Call types represent function invocations and method calls in Metatron.

    Method Call Syntax:

    object.method(args)

    Chained Calls:

    [1,2,3].reverse().merge()

    Function Composition: Functions can be composed using the ring multiplication operator (.):

    f · g               % Compose functions f and g

    Lambda Expressions:

    (x,y){x + y}        % Lambda function

    instructions

    An inst is an obj of the form:

    \begin{align*} \texttt{inst} = \left(\texttt{uri} \times \left( \texttt{poly} \times \texttt{code} \right) \times \texttt{uri} \right), \end{align*}

    where each component is:

    1. uri: the inst type (e.g. /m/inst/plus).

    2. poly: the inst arguments which can either be a lst or a rec.

    3. code: the code that defines the behavior or function of the inst.

    4. uri: the inst location in space.

    variables

    Variables in Metatron can be scoped at different levels: frame stack, thread cache, local, and distributed.

    Variable Assignment and Dereferencing:

    mtron> x -> 42     ​1​
==>42
mtron> *x     ​2​
==>42
mtron> y -> [a=>1,b=>2,c=>3]     ​3​
==>[a=>1,b=>2,c=>3]
mtron> *y     ​4​
==>[a=>1,b=>2,c=>3]
    1 Assign 42 to variable x
    2 Dereference x with *x
    3 Assign record to variable y
    4 Dereference y

    Variables in Computations:

    mtron> base -> 10     ​1​
==>10
mtron> result -> *base.plus(5).mult(2)     ​2​
==>30
mtron> *result     ​3​
==>10
    1 Store base value
    2 Compute and store result
    3 Retrieve computed result

    Variables with Collections:

    mtron> nums -> {1,2,3,4,5}     ​1​
==>1
==>2
==>3
==>4
==>5
mtron> filtered -> *nums-<[?>3 => _, _ => noobj]>>     ​2​
==>fail::[parse error at line 1, col 19:
     filtered -> *nums-<[?>3 => _, _ => noobj]>>
                       ^
     could not parse at '<']@/sys/fail/622
mtron> *filtered     ​3​
    1 Store collection
    2 Filter and store results
    3 Retrieve filtered collection 
        ........─►.age::T.◄─.......
    .            ▲            .
    .            │            .
    ▼            │            ▼
odd::T ....... nat::T ......even::T
    ▲            ▲            ▲
    │            │            │
    │            │            │
    └──────────int::T─────────┘
    mtron> nat  -> int::T[?>0]     ​1​
mtron> age  -> nat::T[?<120]     ​2​
mtron> odd  -> int::T[is(mod(2).eq(1))]     ​3​
mtron> even -> int::T[is(mod(2).eq(0))]     ​4​
    1 A nat is an int greater than 0.
    2 A age is a nat (is an int greater than 0) less than 120.
    3 An odd is an int that is not divisible by 2.
    4 An even is an int that is divisible by 2. 
       age::T
     |   \
   nat::T even::T
     |   /
   int::T
    mtron> age::T[?even::T]

    For instance, it is possible to define the type nat which represents the set of natural numbers — integers greater than zero. In mtron, this is written:

    \[\tt{int::T[?>0]}\]

    The int::T (int type) is being constrained by the predicate ?>0 (or is(gt(0))). This constraint is a refinement of int (a well defined subset).

    mtron> nat -> int::T[?>0]@nat
mtron> nat::10
==>nat::10
mtron>  nat::-10
==>fail::[-10 is not a int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat]@/sys/fail/640
mtron>  int::-10.as(nat::T)
==>fail::[apply failure:
   	[lhs]    │ -10
   	 \_type  │ /m/int
   	  \_pred │ []
   	[inst]   │ as?rng=nat&dom=int(nat::T){<j>}@(1)
   	 \_dom   │ int::T
   	 \_args  │ [nat::T][MTronException<137>:-10 is not a int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat [structural]]][-10 is not a int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat [structural]]@/sys/fail/642

    If a is an obj When a tid is specified for an obj, the tid is resolved to an obj denoting the type of A.

    Every mtron obj has a type.

    metatron objs are typed. Types are organized into instruction sets that encapsulate the structures and processes necessary to effect domain-specific views of the underlying data space. The type system supports refinement types, optional/maybe types, generic/polymorphic types, recursive/self-referential types, dependent types, type cardinality, and more.

    mtron core types

    The fundamental types are organized into the 5 categories presented in the table below.

    kind description examples

    universal types

    noobj::T, #::T

    mono types

    bytes::T, bool::T, int::T, real::T, str::T, uri::T

    poly types

    rel::T, lst::T, rec::T

    call types

    inst::T, code::T

    error types

    fail::T, caught::T

    machine types

    monad::T, machine::T

    A natural number is a positive integer greater than zero. In order to constrain an int to this definition, a refinement type can be constructed.

    mtron> nat -> int::T[?>0]@nat
mtron> nat::10
==>nat::10
mtron>  nat::-10
==>fail::[-10 is not a int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat]@/sys/fail/644
mtron>  int::-10.as(nat::T)
==>fail::[apply failure:
   	[lhs]    │ -10
   	 \_type  │ /m/int
   	  \_pred │ []
   	[inst]   │ as?rng=nat&dom=int(nat::T){<j>}@(1)
   	 \_dom   │ int::T
   	 \_args  │ [nat::T][MTronException<137>:-10 is not a int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat [structural]]][-10 is not a int::T[is(gt(0))]@/m/math/nat [structural]]@/sys/fail/646

    Type inheritance is possible through type chaining. In the example below, the age type has a value id of age, but a type id of nat. Moreover, nat has a value id of nat and a type id of int. In this way, when age is resolved, its associated predicate (?<120) and inherited predicates (?>0) must hold for the respective value to be considered an age.

    mtron> age -> nat::T[?<120]
mtron> age::T
mtron> nat::T
mtron> int::T
    mtron> nat::125
==>fail::[125 is not a int::T[is(lt(120))]@/m/math/nat]@/sys/fail/648
mtron> age::125
==>fail::[125 is not a int::T[is(lt(120))]@/m/math/nat]@/sys/fail/650

    The console offers an integrated user experience. Features include:

    1. mtron syntax highlighting

    There are 3 instructions associated with a /m/mach/console/pane.

    1. prompt(str::T) - write to the pane using the standard prompt prefix mtron> xyz.

    2. result(str::T) - write to the pane using the standard result prefix ==> xyz.

    These functions are useful for creating remote consoles, separating log output, and LLM pair programming, where an LLM can operate with their own environment fully visible to the user.

    Polynomial Data Structures

    • data structures
    • Poly Types
    • Lists
    • Relations
    • Records
    • Control Structures
    • Code Structures
    • Query Structures

    The poly types are one of the more interesting aspects of metatron. They are used to structure data (serving as collections) and for for flow control (code branching). There are 3 poly types in metatron: rec (record), lst (list), and rel (relation) — the linchpins of metatron.

    data structures

    The obvious application of a poly is as the scaffolding for a data structure. A poly is a collection able to bundle multiple objs together. Via the colocation of two objs within the confines of a poly, two objs are bound. Another way to bind two objs together is via explicit uri referencing. This method is a form of lazy binding in which "colocation" is computed through dereferencing, and as such, dangling pointers are possible. With poly, there can be no "dangling pointers" — either two objs are colocated in the poly or not. However, mtron is lenient on lazy pointers. It is important to realize the ramifications of the fail-free aspect of noobj resolved pointers.

    graceful degradation in the void with noobj

    The intended use of metatron is to connect disparate datasets and processors into a cohesive structure bound by loose structural similarities. From resources on the web, to LLMs on networked GPU, to vertices linked within a graph database, data entropy will come to fragment structural bonds as different stakeholders within the larger data infrastructure independently alter their sphere of "data influence." In uncertain data environments, noobj and it’s manifestation in maybe/maybe_some coefficients {?}/{*} make dangling pointers and type assumptions convenient for the user.

    mtron> 1-<[?>0,?>2,*/usr/${_}]     ​1​
==>[1,noobj,noobj]
mtron> 1-<[?>0,?>2,*/usr/${_}]>-     ​2​
==>1
mtron> 1-<[?>0,?>2,*/usr/${_}]>-.map(<${g:V/_}>)>>+     ​3​
==>fail::[apply failure:
   	[lhs]    │ ${g:V/_}
   	 \_type  │ /m/uri
   	  \_pred │ []
   	[inst]   │ rshift?rng=B{*}&dom=A(<+>){<j>}@(4)
   	 \_dom   │ A::T
   	 \_args  │ [<+>][MTronException<137>:unable to parse ${g to a furi: ${g]][unable to parse ${g to a furi: ${g]@/sys/fail/442
    1 dangling pointers that lead to nowhere, lead to noobj.
    2 mtron silently ignores noobj and continues with those objs it knows.
    3 address wildcards (+) are a foothold in a noobj-world.

    Poly Types

    Lists

    Relations

    mtron> a=>b=>c     ​1​
==>a=>(b=>c)
mtron> [a=>{b,c},
        b=>{a,c},
        c=>{a,b}]     ​2​
==>[a=>{b,c},b=>{a,c},c=>{a,b}]
mtron> [a,b,c]     ​3​
==>[a,b,c]
    1 a rel combines two objs, but due to associative nature of the => operator, a rel can bind an obj to another rel.
    2 a rec can be understood as a "bag of `rels`", but this conceptualization looses its hold when considering control and query.
    3 a lst combines objs via positional association.

    rel/traverse vs. to/from

    Consider the following expressions:

    mtron> a=>b=>c     ​1​
==>a=>(b=>c)
mtron> a->b->c     ​2​
==>c
    1 a rel associates a to b=>c and another rel associates b to c.
    2 to?uri<=uri associates a to b and another to?uri<=uri associates b to c. 
    mtron> a=>b=>c.>>     ​1​
==>b=>c
mtron> a=>b=>c.>>.>>     ​2​
==>c
mtron> a->b->c.*a     ​3​
==>b
mtron> a->b->c.**a     ​4​
==>c
    1 >>?#<=rel maps a rel to it’s rng.
    2 Two >> yields the tail of the relational chain, c.
    3 *?#<=uri maps a uri to an obj.
    4 Two ** yields the tail of the referential chain, c.

    The third expression constructs and traverses its data structure on the stack. The fourth expression constructs and traverses its data structure in space. It just so happens that the space that the fourth expression is operating in is the stack. This is due to the fact that <+/#> is the stack’s pattern, and therefore, matches a, b, c.

    mtron> [== a->(b->c) ==]     ​1​
mtron> *a
==>b
mtron> **a
==>c
    1 a references the instruction b→c.
    2 *a resolves to the instruction b→c which, as an auto (!), immediately applies a to b yielding c.
