    The examples highlight important concepts necessary to understand in order to be effective at traversing and manipulating complex, nested structures existing across heterogeneous data sources.

    • -< (copy apply): copy the input obj across all slots of the arg poly.

    • == (match apply): split the input poly to match the slots of the arg poly.

    • >> (project select): select a particular component of the input poly.

    • ${} (template select): process the input obj and output a str representation of the output obj.

    		 
          grph          tble
┌──────────┐    ┌───────────┐
│┌──┐      │    │┌─────────┐│
││  ├──────┼────►├─────────┤│
│└─┬┘      │    │├─────────┤│
│  │       │    │├─────────┤│
│  │  ┌──┐ │    │├─────────┤│
│  └─►│  │◄┼────┤├─────────┤│
│     └──┘ │    │└─────────┘│
└──────────┘    └───────────┘































                              foreign key
                        tableA ┌───────┐ tableB
  v[A]   v[B]  redirect ┌─┬─┬─┬┼┬─┐   ┌┼┬─┬─┬─┐
 ┌───┐   ┌───┐┌────────►├─┼─├─┼┼┼─┤   ├▼┼─┼─┼─┤
 │   │──►│   ├┘         ├─┼─┼─┼─┼─┤   ├─┼─┼─┼─┤
 └───┘   └───┘          └─┴─┴─┴─┴─┘   └─┴─┴─┴─┘
    process encoding

    A vertex can subscribe to events, maintain a thread of execution, and pass obj messages to other vertices in the graph.

    sympathetic partitions

    A vertex’s edges can span multiple storage backends. Hot edges (frequently traversed) live in an in-memory space; cold edges (archival) live in a disk-backed space. The vertex does not know the difference — */g/V/1/OUT/+ returns all edges regardless of where they reside.

    The following example configures the modern TinkerGraph dataset with two additional edge stores: hot: for in-memory edges and cold: for disk-backed edges.

    hot and cold edge storage spaces
    mtron> memspace::[pattern  => </te/hot/#>,
                  route    => [/te/hot/  => <>]]@/sys/space/hot
mtron> tblespace::[pattern => </te/cold/#>,
                   route   => [/te/cold/ => <>],
                   table   => [,],
                   driver  => <org.sqlite.JDBC>,
                   host    => <sqlite:/tmp/cold.db>]@/sys/space/cold

    Edge data is written into each space keyed by the vertex identifier.

    mtron> /te/hot/1/a  -> [item   => 'laptop',
                       total=>1200.0,
                       owner  => !*g:V/2]
==>[
    item=>'laptop',
    total=>1200.0000,
    owner=>!*g:V/2]
mtron> /te/hot/1/b  -> [item   => 'mouse',total=>25.0]
==>[item=>'mouse',total=>25.0000]
mtron> /te/cold/1/x -> [item   => 'book',total=>15.0,
                       seller => !*g:V/4]
==>[
    item=>'book',
    total=>15.0000,
    seller=>!*g:V/4]

    Once seeded, the edges are accessible through the same OUT traversal used for native graph edges. The scheme prefix in the label (te:hot, te:cold) instructs the engine to route the read to the respective space.

    traversing a distributed edge set (path syntax)
    mtron> *g:V/1/OUT/te:hot/+.count()     ​1​

mtron> *g:V/1/OUT/te:hot/a/item     ​2​
==>'laptop'

mtron> *g:V/1.outE(knows).count()     ​5​
==>1
    1 Count of hot edges for vertex 1 — returns 2.
    2 Specific hot edge property value — "laptop".
    3 Passthrough: traverse hot edge → back to graph via !* reference → vertex name — "vadas".
    4 Cold edge property value — 15.0.
    5 Native graph edges coexist with external edges — returns 2.

    The reference !*g:V/2 in the hot edge’s owner field creates an auto_from pointer back to the graph. When accessed via /owner/name, the pointer resolves to vertex 2 in the graph, and its name property returns "vadas". The traversal path graph → external edge → graph is transparent to the user.

    The two syntactic forms — */g/V/1/OUT/te:hot/a/item and *g:V/1.outE(te:hot/a)>>item — are dual representations of the same underlying identifier resolution. The path form decomposes into g:V (collection), 1 (entry), OUT (direction), te:hot (external-space label), a (edge key), and item (field). The instruction form wraps the same decomposition: outE(te:hot/a) encodes direction + label + key, and >>item projects the field. Both route through the graph engine’s readVertexTraversal, where the scheme prefix te: triggers a read from the external memspace. Whether expressed as uri-path segments or instruction calls, the traversal graph ⎡vertex⎦ → ⎡external edge⎦ → ⎡property⎦ is identical — the instructions are simply an alternate notation for the same underlying path resolution.

    metatron Encoded Graphs

    It is possible to encode an arbitrary graph structure (i.e. any extension of \(G=(V,E)\)) in space. There are four aaspects of mtron that make graph encodings natural and universal — applicable in any metatron space, regardless of the underlying storage system.

    • !* (auto_from): ensures that simply accessing a reference yields the reference’s referent. To the user, there is no difference between the graph elements being colocated in the same poly, within the same space, within the same computer, or across the web as a whole.

    • >> (forward): ensures monads move along the underlying data structure in a manner respective of the provided argument.

    • -< (split): ensures that executing monads will split themselves across branching graph paths and branching code paths.

    • >- (merge): ensures that branching graph and code paths will linearize themselves within the execution path of the machine.

    directed single-relational binary graph

    A directed single-relational binary graph connects vertices by binary edges (a single tail and a single head vertex), where all edges have the same label/meaning (single-relational). An example of such a graph is presented in the code and diagram below.

    mtron> v1 -> [!*v2, !*v3, !*v4]
==>[!*v2,!*v3,!*v4]
mtron> v2 -> [,]
==>[,]
mtron> v3 -> [!*v4, !*v5]
==>[!*v4,!*v5]
mtron> v4 -> [,]
==>[,]
mtron> v5 -> [,]
==>[,]
mtron> v6 -> [!*v4]
==>[!*v4]
mtron> v1     ​1​
==>v1
mtron> *v1     ​2​
==>[!*v2,!*v3,!*v4]
mtron> *v1.>>
==>{2}[,]
==>[!*v4,!*v5]
mtron> *v1.>>.>>
==>{2}[,]
    1 v1 denotes the source vertex (0-step traversal).
    2 *v1 references the adjacent vertices to v1 (1-step traversal).
    3 *v1>> traversers the v1 incident edges to the v1 adjanct vertices.
    4 *v1>>.>> traveres the those edges emanating from the 3 adjacent vertices to v1. 
                  ┌────┐
          ┌───► v3 ◄───┐
          │   └───▲┘   │     ┌────┐
 ┌────┐   │       │    └─────│ v5 │
 │ v1 │───┘       │          └────┘
 └────┘           │
  │  │            │
  │  │           ┌┴───┐
  │  └───────────│ v4 │
  │              └─┬──┘
  │                │
  │ ┌────┐         │          ┌────┐
  └─► v2 │         └──────────► v6 │
    └────┘                    └────┘

    To achieve a similar look and feel to Gremlin syntax, an out instruction can be defined to perform the respective >>.>> concatenation.

    Performance Tiering

    file system in-memory database 
    fsspace::[pattern=>fs:#]
    		 
    memspace::[pattern=>mem:#]
    		 
    dcmtspace::[pattern=>dcmt:#]

    • slow

    • static

    • stable.

    • fast

    • dynamic

    • transient.

    • fast

    • complex

    • stable

    A typical configuration partitions subgraphs by access frequency. Frequently traversed knows-edges reside in memspace (sub-millisecond reads). Rarely queried purchases-edges reside in tblespace (disk-backed). The vertex’s OUT traversal remains a single expression regardless of how many spaces contribute edges.

    Machines

    • Swarm Machine

    Every mtron expression is evaluated by a machine. There are various types of machines, each optimized for a particular data processing scenario. Every machine is refinement of virtual::T (a virtual thread).

    mtron> *virtual
mtron> *machine

    The consequence of this type dependence is many fold:

    1. pause/resume: every machine can be paused mid-computation, manipulated/serialized, and restarted.

    2. source: every machine has a source thread that spawned its creation. This source can be another machine (in the case of nested expressions) or a thread (the thread of execution).

    3. call stack: the data structure formed by the !* source relations yields a nested call stack that can be walked in a manner aligned with debugging tools.

    4. pure state: the machine’s state, when separated from its thread of execution is a rec of objs. The relations of the machine include its running monads, its barrier monads, and its halted monads. Thus, the full state of the computation is contained within the machine (where !* is considered containment in simulated co-located sense),

    Swarm Machine

    A swarm::T realizes the computation as a "swarm" of independent, autonomous monads moving over the insts of the machine’s code and, as dictated by the code, over the objs in space. The only coordination that exists in swarm::T is at _barrier instructions. A barrier aggregates all monads up to that point, and when no monads are left outside the barrier, the barrier then drains the monads — allowing them to once again independently execute.

    mtron> {1,2,3}-<[+1,+2,+3]>-.sum()
==>36

    Threads and Coroutines

    • Type Structure
    • Thread Refinements
    • Spawning Threads
    • Yielding Coroutines
    • Pausing and Resuming
    • The Main Core Thread

    mtron’s threading model exposes two concurrent primitives — virtual and core — both subtypes of thread. Every thread is a first-class obj in the URI space, queryable, pausable, and traceable through its source lineage. The types involved in threading are thread (unrefined type) and its two refinements: virtual and core.

    mtron> *virtual
mtron> *core

    Type Structure

    key type description

    code

    #{*}::T

    the obj (typically code) evaluated each iteration (start is lhs)

    source

    thread::T

    !* reference to the parent thread

    loop

    time{?}::T

    time::T between repeated evaluations; omit for single execution

    state

    uri::T

    run (executing), pause (suspended), or stop (completed)

    result

    #{*}::T

    the last obj produced by code(start) (can change after every loop)

    start

    #{?}::T

    initial input obj, set by the spawning expression

    yield

    thread{?}::T

    !* reference to a coroutine partner — resumed when this thread pauses

    Thread Refinements

    A virtual thread is a lightweight, cooperative concurrency primitive backed by a Java virtual thread. It shares CPU resources with other virtual threads and is suitable for IO-bound work and high-concurrency workloads.

    A core thread is bound to an OS platform thread from the executor’s pool. It is appropriate for CPU-bound work where you want dedicated execution resources.

    Both types share identical semantics — the same keys, the same lifecycle, the same query surface. The difference is purely in the underlying execution substrate.

    Threads are addressable through the /sys/thread uri space. The thread executor exposes two lst objs — run and stop — for live and completed threads.

    mtron> */sys/thread/executor/run/0     ​1​
mtron> */sys/thread==<+>==run==<+>.vid() * <.?docq>.*_     ​2​
    1 state of the first running thread
    2 long running system threads may have associated documentation

    Thread State Machine

                    ┌─────────┐
       apply()  │         │
NEW ──────────► │   RUN   │ ◄──────── resume()
                │         │─────────┐
                └─────────┘         │
                     │   ▲          │ pause()
                stop │   │ resume   │
                     ▼   │          ▼
                ┌─────────┐    ┌─────────┐
                │  STOP   │    │  PAUSE  │
                └─────────┘    └─────────┘

    A thread begins in run when constructed. Calling pause() sets state⇒pause — the current cycle completes first, then the thread parks. resume() wakes it. stop() (or a break from the stop check) terminates it. A one-shot thread (no loop) executes once and transitions directly to stop.

    Spawning Threads

    Threads are constructed inline with their type constructor and started immediately:

    virtual::[code=>1+2]                           [-- <1> --]
virtual::[code=>print('.'), loop=>second::5.0] [-- <2> --]
core::[code=>expensive_computation]            [-- <3> --]
    1 executes 1+2 once, stores 3 in result
    2 prints a dot every 5 seconds, looping until stopped
    3 runs CPU-heavy work on a platform thread 
    mtron> */sys/thread/executor/run
==>[,]
mtron> */sys/thread/executor/stop
==>[!*/sys/thread/main]
mtron> */sys/thread/executor/stop/+/source
mtron> */sys/thread/executor/stop/+/source/source

    Yielding Coroutines

    A thread with yield⇒!*/sys/thread/<vid> forms a cooperative pair. When one pauses, it auto-resumes the other. Both threads must exist before their yield pointers can reference each other — construct the partner paused first, then link them:

    mtron> ""@ab     ​1​
==>''@ab
mtron> virtual::[code  => @ab + 'B',
                 loop  => second::1.0,
                 state => pause]@/sys/thread/b     ​3​
==>virtual::[
    code=>at(ab).plus('B'),
    loop=>second::1.0000,
    state=>run,
    source=>!*/sys/thread/main,
    start=>[
     code=>at(ab).plus('B'),
     loop=>second::1.0000,
     state=>pause]@/sys/thread/b]@/sys/thread/b
mtron> virtual::[code  => @ab + 'A',
                 loop  => second::1.0,
                 yield => !*/sys/thread/b]@/sys/thread/a     ​4​
==>virtual::[
    code=>at(ab).plus('A'),
    loop=>second::1.0000,
    yield=>!*/sys/thread/b,
    source=>!*/sys/thread/main,
    start=>[
     code=>at(ab).plus('A'),
     loop=>second::1.0000,
     yield=>!*/sys/thread/b]@/sys/thread/a,
    state=>run]@/sys/thread/a
mtron> @/sys/thread/b >>= +[yield => !*/sys/thread/a]     ​5​
==>virtual::[
    code=>at(ab).plus('B'),
    loop=>second::1.0000,
    state=>run,
    source=>!*/sys/thread/main,
    start=>[
     code=>at(ab).plus('B'),
     loop=>second::1.0000,
     state=>pause]@/sys/thread/b,
    result=>'B'@ab]@/sys/thread/b
mtron> sleep(second::5.0)
mtron> */sys/thread/b.stop()
==>virtual::[
    code=>at(ab).plus('B'),
    loop=>second::1.0000,
    state=>stop,
    source=>!*/sys/thread/main,
    start=>[
     code=>at(ab).plus('B'),
     loop=>second::1.0000,
     state=>pause]@/sys/thread/b,
    result=>'BABABABAB'@ab]@/sys/thread/b
mtron> */sys/thread/a.stop()
==>virtual::[
    code=>at(ab).plus('A'),
    loop=>second::1.0000,
    yield=>!*/sys/thread/b,
    source=>!*/sys/thread/main,
    start=>[
     code=>at(ab).plus('A'),
     loop=>second::1.0000,
     yield=>!*/sys/thread/b]@/sys/thread/a,
    state=>stop,
    result=>'BABABABABABA'@ab]@/sys/thread/a
mtron> *ab
==>'BABABABABABA'
    1 partner B starts paused
    2 initiator A points to B, begins running
    3 direct B yield at A — the pair is now linked

    A appends A to the str@ab, pauses, yields to B. B appends B to the same str::T, pauses, then yields to A. The two trade turns executing their code, where one full cycle completes approximately every 2 seconds.

    Pausing and Resuming

    Any looping thread can be paused mid-execution. The current cycle always completes first.

    mtron> virtual::[code=>print('.'),loop=>second::2.5]@/sys/thread/example     ​1​
==>virtual::[
    code=>print('.'),
    loop=>second::2.5000,
    source=>!*/sys/thread/main,
    start=>[code=>print('.'),loop=>second::2.5000]@/sys/thread/example,
    state=>run]@/sys/thread/example
mtron> */sys/thread/example.pause()     ​2​
==>virtual::[
    code=>print('.'),
    loop=>second::2.5000,
    source=>!*/sys/thread/main,
    start=>[code=>print('.'),loop=>second::2.5000]@/sys/thread/example,
    state=>pause,
    result=>[code=>print('.'),loop=>second::2.5000]@/sys/thread/example]@/sys/thread/example
mtron> */sys/thread/example/state     ​3​
==>pause
mtron> */sys/thread/example.resume()     ​4​
==>virtual::[
    code=>print('.'),
    loop=>second::2.5000,
    source=>!*/sys/thread/main,
    start=>[code=>print('.'),loop=>second::2.5000]@/sys/thread/example,
    state=>run,
    result=>[code=>print('.'),loop=>second::2.5000]@/sys/thread/example]@/sys/thread/example
mtron> */sys/thread/example/state     ​5​
==>run
    1 Create a simple looping virtual thread
    2 Pause the simple looping virtual thread
    3 Check the state of the simple looping virtual thread
    4 Resume the simple looping virtual thread
    5 Check the state of the simple looping virtual thread

    Non-looping threads (no loop key) cannot be paused — a warning is logged and the call is a no-op.

    The Main Core Thread

    At boot, metatron establishes a root thread at /sys/thread/main. All user-spawned threads trace back to this root via source. The root thread blocks on the shutdown latch — when metatron shuts down, it transitions to stop.

    mtron> */sys/thread/main
==>core::[
    code=>inst?#{*}<=#{?}(#{*}::T),
    state=>stop]
mtron> */sys/thread/main?docq
==>docs::[
    obj=>core::[
     code=>inst?#{*}<=#{?}(#{*}::T),
     state=>stop],
    desc=>'this root thread waits till all child threads are complete ...']

    Web Technologies

    • HTTP Space
    • HTML Type
    • Accessing REST APIs
    • WebSocket Space
    • Remote Console
    • The `mimeq` Query Processor
    • Content-Type Negotiation
    web technologies setup 
    mtron>  import(/m/web)
...
mtron>  import(/m/llm)
...
mtron> catalog::[host         => <http://localhost:1234/v1>,
                 pattern      => <lmstudio:#>,
                 name         => <openai>,
                 route        => [<lmstudio:>=><>]]@/sys/space/lmstudio
==>catalog::[
    host=>http://localhost:1234/v1,
    pattern=>lmstudio:#,
    name=>openai,
    route=>[lmstudio:=><>]]@/sys/space/lmstudio

    The /m/web instruction set enables a wide range of capabilities centered around http-based systems and websocket-based systems. Correspondingly, there exists httpspace and wsspace that manage respective network i/o connections. Along with these spaces are various serializers that provide structural encoding/decoding of common web document standards such as html, xml, json, markdown, yaml, and the like.

    Web document types (html::T, json::T, xml::T, markdown::T, etc.) refine str::T — the MIME type acts as a Predicate on the string content (e.g., html::T validates that the string is well-formed HTML). The structural rec::T representation is opt-in via .as(rec::T) or the ?mimeq=application/x-mtron query processor.

    HTTP Space

    The httpspace::T is a space capable of spawning and managing http connection handlers both as servers and as clients. The pattern of an httpspace dictates the breadth of resources accessible. When http://# is the pattern, the world wide web becomes a source of metatron objs.

    mtron> httpspace::[
        host     => http://localhost:8777,
        pattern  => http://#,
        route    => [/        => <>,
                     /mcp     => mcp_mtron_http,
                     /docs    => target/docs,
                     /console => src/main/resources/web/console]
       ]@/sys/space/web/http
==>fail::[unable to construct httpspace::T: fail::[apply failure:
   	[lhs]    │ [
    host=>http://localhost:8777,
    pattern=>http://#,
    route=>[
     /=><>,
     /mcp=>mcp_mtron_http,
     /docs=>target/docs,
     /console=>src/main/resources/web/console]]@/sys/space/web/http
   	 \_type  │ /m/rec
   	  \_pred │ []
   	[inst]   │ /m/web/space/httpspace/ctor?rng=httpspace&dom=#{?}([
     host=>http://localhost:8777,
     pattern=>http://#,
     route=>[
      /=><>,

    HTML Type

    The type html::T < str::T (a refinement of str::T) represents an HTML document. The MIME type acts as a Predicate — html::T validates that the content is well-formed HTML. The structural rec::T DOM tree is opt-in.

    1 html::T refines str::T — a typed string with predicate validation.
    2 Default read returns an html::"…​" typed string.
    3 Cast to rec::T via .as(rec::T) to get the DOM tree structure.

    Accessing REST APIs

    When a REST endpoint has a URL address that matches an httpspace pattern, the REST resources can be used in metatron. This is possible because httpspace transforms data "in the wild" (e.g. application/json) into an isomorphic obj. If the newly mapped obj is typed, then it inherits the instructions for that type as defined by the instset of the type. As such, REST resources can be enriched/typed to become active computational objs in metatron.

    The Ollama endpoint will serve to demonstrate how to leverage foreign resources in metatron.

    mtron> *<http://localhost:11434>     ​1​

    Once the desired REST resource has been accessed, it’s possible to map the resource into a form that meets the structural requirements of an mtron type. In doing so, the resource then acquires the behaviors defined in the type’s respective instset. For this example, that instset is /m/llm.

    mtron> */m/llm/model
mtron> */m/llm/inst/+?dom=model
    1 The model::T type in /m/llm.
    2 The /m/llm instructions with a domain of model::T (an object oriented perspective — obj.f()).

    When the REST resource is manipulated to fit the structural specification of model::T, then http://localhost:11434/api/tags can be the source of chat?#<=model.

    mtron> *<http://localhost:11434/api/tags?mimeq=application/x-mtron>/0.>>data/1.
         -<[provider=> ollama,
            protocol=> ollama,
            host    => <http://localhost:11434>,
            name    => >>id.as(uri::T)].as(model::T)
         .chat('are you there?')
==>fail::[parse error at line 1, col 61:
     ...1434/api/tags?mimeq=application/x-mtron>/0.>>data/1.
            -<[provider=> olla...
                                                ^
     could not parse at '/']@/sys/fail/712
    1 The OpenAPI application/json resource is morphed into a metatron model::T via ?mimeq=application/x-mtron for structural access, and then the model is chatted with.

    WebSocket Space

    The wsspace::T is similar to httpspace::T, but enables long running connections and bidirectional communication. A particular useful aspect of wsspace::T is for communication between two metatron instances.

    Remote Console

    If another metatron instance is running on a remote machine, it’s possible to route all console input to the remote metatron for evaluation. In order to make the experience seamless for the user, the redirect/input relation of console::T can be used.

    In the following example, the "remote" metatron instance is localhost. This is because all code examples in this documentation are evaluated when the documentation is compiled. Note that localhost can be set to any hostname or IP address that is running a metatron instance with a wsspace::T exposing the mtron_ws::T handler. 
    mtron> wsclient::[host=><ws://localhost:8555/mtron>]@/usr/ui/console/remote     ​1​
==>wsclient::[host=>ws://localhost:8555/mtron]@/usr/ui/console/remote
    1 creates a websocket connection to a remote metatron instance with a wsspace::T exposing an mtron_ws::T route.

    In the local console UI (the REPL), use the :redirect/input metacommand to have all user input processed by an arbitrary inst. When that inst is a wsclient::T.send_recv(), then a remote console is effected.

    mtron> :redirect/input inst?#{*}<=#{?}(to_send=>#{?}::T){*/usr/ui/console/remote.send_recv(*to_send)}
[INFO ] [/usr/ui/console] redirecting console input to inst?#{*}<=#{?}(to_send=>#{?}::T){*/usr/ui/console/remote.send_recv(*to_send)}

    At this point, all console input is sent to the remote metatron instance, evaluated at that instance via the mtron_ws::T endpoint, where the results are returned to the local instance and displayed in the local console UI.

    To have REPL input evaluated locally (the default behavior), set the console::T.redirect/input to noobj.

    mtron> :redirect/input noobj
[INFO ] [/usr/ui/console] redirecting console input to noobj
mtron> :redirect/input
[INFO ] [/usr/ui/console] console input currently redirected to noobj

    The console::T.redirect/input can be any inst. The flexibility of such redirection allows any number of use cases such as broadcasting, local processing w/ side effects, and more.

    The mimeq Query Processor

    The ?mimeq= query parameter on file and HTTP URIs controls how content is read. It is implemented by the mimeq query processor (QCollection.mimeQ()) which must be mounted on the space:

    fsspace::[q => [mimeq::[=>]], ...]   # mount on fsSpace
httpspace::[q => [mimeq::[=>]], ...] # mount on httpSpace

    The mimeq postRead processor operates in two steps:

    1. Probe & Tag — determines the actual content MIME type from the object’s TID (or falls back to URI/file extension), then tags the string with the correct type TID. This triggers Predicate validation (e.g., html::T validates the string is well-formed HTML).

    2. Structural Parse — if ?mimeq=application/x-mtron is requested, runs the content-type-specific serializer (ObjHTMLSerializer for HTML, ObjJSONSerializer for JSON, etc.) to produce the rec::T structural representation.

    [-- default: typed string --]
*<local:index.html>
==>html::"<html>...</html>"

[-- structural parse via mimeq --]
*<local:index.html?mimeq=application/x-mtron>
==>[html => [head => [...], body => [...]]]

[-- explicit type tagging (no structural parse) --]
*<local:data.json?mimeq=text/html>  # tags as html, even for .json file

    Content-Type Negotiation

    The in and out query parameters on websocket connections specify the serialization format for messages. application/x-mtron is the native mtron wire format; application/json produces standard JSON.

    $ websocat "ws://localhost:8555/mtron?in=application/mtron&out=application/json"
[a=>[b=>[1,2,3],c=>2]]
{"a":{"b":[1,2,3],"c":2}}

    Document Databases

    • Typing Documents
    • Inter-Space References
    /m/dcmnt instset import 
    mtron> import(/m/dcmnt)

    A document database models a domain as a set of "JSON"-ish documents — in the lexicon of formality, such databases are understood as a collection of associative arrays/dictionary/hashmap composed of string keys and object values, where values can be nested indefinitely. In metatron, a document database is best captured by a dcmntspace of type rec::T. The mapping between JSON and metatron is quite natural. Two semantically equivalent structures are provided below.

    JSON mtron 
    { "databases" : {
  "graphdb": {"vertices":10234,"edges":1234545 },
  "docdb": {"collections": 2, "documents":{
   "users": 102,
   "data": 234335
  }},
  "tabledb": {"tables": 2,"rows": [1334,34345]}
}}
    		 
    [ databases => [
  graphdb => [vertices => 10234, edges => 1234545],
  docdb => [collections => 2, documents => [
    users => 102,
    data => 234335
  ]],
  tabledb => [tables => 2, rows => [1334,34345]]
]]

    uri keys vs str keys

    one nicety that immediately jumps out when comparing JSON and mtron is mtron’s lack of quotes. this is because the mtron keys are not strings, but URIs. it is typically best to use URIs over strings for the following reasons:

    • URIs are less verbose as they don’t require quotes.

    • URIs have a well-defined hierarchical structure that is useful for referencing nested structures.

    • URIs support wildcards and templates for pattern matching and bulk read/writes.

    mtron> */m/dcmnt/space/dcmntspace

    when creating a dcmntspace, it is useful to define a schema. a schema is simply an instset by another name. a type from the schema is selected to be the root. for schemaless behavior (where arbitrary collections can be created), the root should simply be rec::T. should the database enforce closed-world semantics, the root is rec::T[?[collection_name=>collection_type{*}::T]].

    		 
    mtron> *instset
    		 
    mtron> animal    -> rec::T[?[name=>str::T,  age=>int::T]]@animal     ​1​
mtron> chicken   -> animal::T@chicken     ​2​
mtron> equipment -> rec::T[?[model=>uri::T, year=>int::T]]@equipment     ​3​
mtron> farmset::[type=>[animal::T,equipment::T,chicken::T]]@/m/dcmnt/schema/farmset     ​4​
==>farmset::[type=>[
    animal::T,
    equipment::T,
    chicken::T]]@/m/dcmnt/schema/farmset
    1 an animal::T < rec::T (refinement type) is defined.
    2 a chicken::T == animal::T (nominal type) is defined.
    3 an equipment::T < rec::T (refinement type) is defined.
    4 a farmset instset is defined containing animal::T, equipment::T, and chicken::T. 
    mtron> dcmntspace::[pattern     => farm:#,
                    host        => mongodb://localhost:27017/farm,
                    schema      => !*farmset,
                    root        => rec::T[?[animals   => animal{*}::T,
                                            equipment => equipment{*}::T]],
                    route       => [farm:=><>]]@/sys/space/farm
    1 a dcmntspace with farmset as the schema. the root type maps each collection to its element type with {*} cardinality. 
    mtron>  farm:animals/1 -> 'a chicken named trill'     ​1​
==>fail::[space /sys/space/farm requires rec::T[?[{?}animals=>animals::T]] at root; got str::T]@/sys/fail/48
mtron> farm:animals/1 -> chicken::[name=>'trill',age=>7]     ​2​
==>chicken::[name=>'trill',age=>7]
mtron> *farm:animals/1     ​3​
==>chicken::[name=>'trill',age=>7]
    1 writing a str::T to a collection that expects animal{*}::T — rejected at the space level.
    2 a chicken::T document satisfies animal::T (chicken is-a animal) — accepted.
    3 reading the document back.

    Typing Documents

    BSON (MongoDB’s binary JSON format) has no concept of a type—​for example, chicken::[name=>'trill', age=>7] stored in MongoDB would ordinarily come back as a plain rec::[name=>'trill', age=>7], losing the chicken::T identity. metatron preserves type information transparently on the way in and restores it on the way out.

    mtron> farm:animals/1 -> chicken::[name=>'trill',age=>7]     ​1​
==>chicken::[name=>'trill',age=>7]
mtron> *farm:animals/1     ​2​
==>chicken::[name=>'trill',age=>7]
mtron> *farm:animals/1/name     ​3​
==>'trill'
    1 write a chicken::T document to the animals collection.
    2 read it back — chicken::T is preserved through the round-trip.
    3 subfield reads navigate into the document without loading the whole record.
    4 a branch read lists all documents in animals as a uri=>obj map.

    An auto_from cell in a dcmntspace document is stored as a native MongoDB DBRef — $ref carries the collection (or scheme:collection for external targets) and $id carries the document id. No metatron-specific footprint is left in the stored data.

    Inter-Space References

    A dcmntspace cell can carry an !* pointer to any URI in metatron. Below, a loc: memspace holds barn information, and farm:animals/1 (the chicken named Trill) is updated with a shelter cell pointing at loc:barns/1 — a cross-space reference. On write, the serializer encodes the external pointer as $ref: "loc:barns" in MongoDB; on read, the loc: scheme is detected and the reference is routed to the memspace.

    mtron> memspace::[pattern => loc:#]@/sys/space/loc     ​1​
mtron> loc:barns/1 -> [label=>'old_red_barn',capacity=>12]     ​2​
==>[label=>'old_red_barn',capacity=>12]
mtron> farm:animals/1 >>= [shelter=>!*loc:barns/1]     ​3​
==>[shelter=>!*loc:barns/1]
mtron> *farm:animals/1     ​4​
==>chicken::[name=>'trill',age=>7]
mtron> *farm:animals/1/shelter     ​5​
mtron> farm:animals/2 -> chicken::[name=>'cluck',age=>3,shelter=>!*loc:barns/1]     ​6​
==>chicken::[
    name=>'cluck',
    age=>3,
    shelter=>!*loc:barns/1]
mtron> *farm:animals/2     ​7​
==>chicken::[
    name=>'cluck',
    age=>3,
    shelter=>!*loc:barns/1]
mtron> *farm:animals/2/shelter     ​8​
==>[label=>'old_red_barn',capacity=>12]
    1 a loc: memspace serves as the cross-space target — any space type (grphSpace, tbleSpace, etc.) works the same way.
    2 write the barn record into loc: memspace.
    3 update the existing animals/1 document (>>=) with a shelter reference — !*loc:barns/1 is an auto_from pointer.
    4 read the updated document — shelter renders as !*loc:barns/1 with the loc: scheme intact (not rewritten to farm:).
    5 traversing the cross-space FK: the shelter path segment forces auto_from to read from loc:barns/1 via the router, returning the full barn record.
    6 create a second animal with both a nominal chicken::T type and a cross-space reference in a single write.
    7 both the type and the reference are preserved through the BSON round-trip.
    8 the same shelter traversal resolves identically — cross-space routing is transparent to the reader.

    bson dbref encoding

    metatron serializes auto_from references as native MongoDB DBRef documents. A DBRef is a plain BSON {$ref, $id} object (no custom fields, no proprietary envelope) so collections with references remain fully compatible with external MongoDB tooling.

    intra-space references store a bare collection name in $ref and the document id in $id:

    {
  "_id": "trill",
  "name": "Trill",
  "age": 7,
  "shelter": { "$ref": "barns", "$id": 1 }
}

    inter-space references prefix the $ref field with the target scheme — the colon is the discriminator that tells the serializer to route through metatron’s router rather than the local database:

    {
  "_id": "trill",
  "name": "Trill",
  "age": 7,
  "shelter": { "$ref": "loc:barns", "$id": 1 }
}

    $id can be an ObjectId (24-char hex), a string, or most numeric types — the serializer auto-detects the format and the deserializer recovers the original id value. $db is deliberately left unused so that existing MongoDB drivers interpret cross-space refs as ordinary references to a collection named "loc:barns" (valid under MongoDB naming rules) until metatron traverses them.

    The loc: prefix in $ref is the only place metatron stores cross-space routing information. There is no _mtron_meta table, no side-channel metadata, and no proprietary BSON type. The reference is a standard DBRef — metatron simply reads the $ref field and checks for a colon. If none is found, the space-local pattern is used; if one is found, the reference is routed to the named scheme’s space.

    Relational Databases

    • Schema Discovery
    • Relational Database Rewrites
    • auto_from as Foreign Keys
    • The _mtron_meta Table
    • Intra- and Inter-Space Discrimination
    • sql footprint
    documentation setup 
    mtron> import(/m/tble)

    A relational database (SQL) stores structured data in named tables, where each table is a collection of typed rows. In metatron, a relational database is a tblespace backed by any JDBC-compliant driver. Each table maps to a rec::T refinement whose rel::T elements correspond to columns. The instset schema is auto-generated at connection time from live database metadata. As of the time of this writing, metatron has been tested against SQLite, PostgreSQL, MariaDB, and MySQL.

    creating a tblespace requires a JDBC host URI, a driver, and a route. Finally, the pattern determines which subset of the URI address space that this space is responsible for.

    		 
    mtron> *tblespace
    		 
    mtron> tblespace::[pattern => lite:#,
                   host    => <sqlite:target/test>,
                   driver  => <org.sqlite.JDBC>,
                   route   => [lite:=><>]]@/sys/space/lite     ​1​
mtron> lite:person/1 -> [name=>'marko', age=>29]     ​2​
==>[name=>'marko',age=>29]
mtron> lite:person/2 -> [name=>'grant', age=>25]     ​3​
==>[name=>'grant',age=>25]
mtron> *lite:person     ​4​
mtron> *lite:person/+/name     ​5​
==>'grant'
==>'alex'
==>'marko'
mtron> *lite:person/+/id     ​6​
==>1
==>3
==>2
    1 a tblespace is mounted at /sys/space/lite and is responsible for objs in lite:#.
    2 writing a row to the person table (table created on first write).
    3 writing a second row.
    4 listing all rows in the person table.
    5 querying the person table — all rows with any name value.
    6 primary keys are encoded in the vid and omitted from the row body — but still addressable as an explicit path segment.
    auto-generated dynamic schemas

    When the first row is added to a table a schema is inferred from the row’s relations. Said in terms of metatron, when the first rec is written to a tblespace collection, the collection’s type is inferred.

    [INFO ] [/sys/space/lite] created table person with 3 columns and primary keys [id]
    mtron> *lite:+

    When a new row is inserted that has more fields than the first row, the tblespace will perform an ALTER TABLE to accommodate the extending data. This, in turn, updates the collection’s type.

    mtron> lite:person/3 -> [name=>'alex', age=>25, skill=>graph]
==>[name=>'alex',age=>25,skill=>graph]
mtron> *lite:+

    Schema Discovery

    On connection, tblespace introspects the live database and generates an instset schema. Each discovered table is some refinement on rec{\*}::T. In the rubric of relational databses, this means zero or more rows in a table, where the rec::T rel::T elements refer to the table column name and respective cell value.

    mtron> *lite:*
    		 
    ┌──────────────────────────────┐
│┌── k0   k1   k2   k3   k4    │r
r│  ┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐│ │e
e│  │v0 ││v1 ││v2 ││v3 ││v4 ││ │c
l└─ └───┘└───┘└───┘└───┘└───┘├─┘
    ┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐│
    │v0 ││v1 ││v2 ││v3 ││v4 ││t
    └───┘└───┘└───┘└───┘└───┘│a
    ┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐│b
    │v0 ││v1 ││v2 ││v3 ││v4 ││l
    └───┘└───┘└───┘└───┘└───┘│e
    ┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐│
    │v0 ││v1 ││v2 ││v3 ││v4 ││
    └───┘└───┘└───┘└───┘└───┘│
    ┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐┌───┐│
    │v0 ││v1 ││v2 ││v3 ││v4 ││
    └───┘└───┘└───┘└───┘└───┘

    A Relational Key/Value Store

    tblespace is a hybrid space. URIs that match a discovered table (db:person/1) route to the SQL table via a respective read/write path. URIs that don’t match a table (db:kv/mykey) fall through to a general-purpose key/value store backed by the same database.

    mtron> lite:kv/single -> 651.02
==>651.0200
mtron> *lite:kv/single
==>651.0200

    When the obj is a nested rec, the relational key/value store becomes a document store.

    mtron> lite:kv/nest -> [a=>[b=>[c=>2,d=>!*lite:kv/nest]]]
==>[a=>[b=>[c=>2,d=>!*lite:kv/nest]]]
mtron> *lite:kv/nest
==>[a=>[b=>[c=>2,d=>!*lite:kv/nest]]]
mtron> *lite:kv/nest/a/b/+
==>[a=>[b=>[c=>2,d=>!*lite:kv/nest]]]
==>2
mtron> *lite:kv/nest/a/b/d
==>[a=>[b=>[c=>2,d=>!*lite:kv/nest]]]

    Relational Database Rewrites

    Regardless of the language, when querying data, the fundamental primitives of the expression ultimately boil down to a collection of aggregate, filter, project, update, etc. type operations. The universality of these operations across programming and query languages ensure that what can be expressed in mtron can also be expressed in SQL and vice versa (almost). Since relational databases are optimized for processing SQL, it’s best to leverage the backend’s SQL-query engine whenever possible. The rewrite insts of /m/tble do just that. In general, all rewrites are instructions of the form:

    \[ \tt rewrite?code\{?\}⇐code(arg_0,arg_1,\ldots,arg_n). \]

    That is, a rewrite inst takes code (lhs), some rewrite-specific parameters (args), and returns code or noobj (code{?}). The lhs code emerges the rhs code. A semantically equivalent form. But then the process continues. Rewrite after rewrite, sculpting the structural embodiment of a process into a leaner, faster, more efficient form until at some point in time, it stops — the code has reached a stable state distribution. Immune to mutation, timeless by definition, it is ready to be fed to a machine that will use it to guide itself through space in search of a solution it was designed to find. Its destiny awaits.

            ┌───────────────────insts────────────────────────┐rewrites
        ┌───────┐ ┌──────┐ ┌───────┐ ┌───────┐ ┌───────┐ │sql_where
code    └───────┘ └──────┘ └───────┘ └───────┘ └───────┘ │
        ┌────────────────┐ ┌───────┐ ┌───────┐ ┌───────┐ │sql_where_count
code''  └────────────────┘ └───────┘ └───────┘ └───────┘ │
        ┌────────────────┐ ┌───────┐ ┌─────────────────┐ │sql_select
code''' └────────────────┘ └───────┘ └─────────────────┘ ▼
    mtron> *lite:person/+.=?=[age=>?<30]     ​1​
==>[name=>'marko',age=>29]@lite:person/noobj
==>[name=>'grant',age=>25]@lite:person/noobj
==>[name=>'alex',age=>25,skill=>graph]@lite:person/noobj
mtron> *lite:person/+/name.count()     ​2​
==>3
mtron> *lite:person/+.==[name=>_]     ​3​
==>[name=>'alex']
==>[name=>'grant']
==>[name=>'marko']
    1 all people under 30 — rewritten to SELECT * FROM person WHERE age < 30.
    2 count of distinct names — rewritten to SELECT COUNT(name) FROM person.
    3 project to name only — rewritten to SELECT name FROM person.

    Finally, like other database-backed spaces, the backend’s native query engine is accessible via a space-specific inst.

    mtron> */sys/space/lite.sql("SELECT name,age FROM person WHERE age < 30")     ​1​
==>[name=>'marko',age=>29]
==>[name=>'grant',age=>25]
==>[name=>'alex',age=>25]
mtron> */sys/space/lite.sql("SELECT name,age FROM person WHERE age < 30")>>age.sum()     ​2​
==>79
    1 sql?rec{*}<=tblespace(str::T) yields zero or more rec objs.
    2 these recs can be further processed by mtron.

    The example above demonstrates how one can move between languages within the same expression as the universal encoding is that of objs in a unified URI address space.

    auto_from as Foreign Keys

    In a relational database, rows can be linked in one of two ways:

    1. procedural (where): a runtime process returns implicit relationships.

    2. structural (foreign key): a lookup returns explicit relationships.

    A foreign key in a table cell denotes a reference to a cell in another table. The foreign key states an explicit relationship between two rows. This is analogous to a graph database’s edges making direct, explicit relationships between vertices ("rows"). A where clause comparing columns across two tables computes adhoc relationships between rows. This is analogous to a graph database’s traversals computing new semantic relationships through adhoc walks.

    Two new tables extend the running example to show both patterns. score holds a plain-string player column whose values happen to match person.name — the link is purely algorithmic, enforced only at query time. award holds a !* pointer directly in a recipient cell — the link is structural and immediately navigable.

    mtron> @/sys/space/lite >>= [table=>[person,score]]     ​1​
mtron> lite:score/1 -> [player=>'marko', points=>42]     ​2​
==>[player=>'marko',points=>42]
mtron> lite:score/2 -> [player=>'grant', points=>99]     ​3​
==>[player=>'grant',points=>99]
mtron> *lite:score/+.=?=[player=>'marko']     ​4​
==>[player=>'marko',points=>42]@lite:score/noobj
mtron> */sys/space/lite.sql("SELECT p.name,p.age,s.points FROM person p JOIN score s ON p.name=s.player")     ​5​
==>[name=>'marko',age=>29,points=>42]
==>[name=>'grant',age=>25,points=>99]
    1 patch the live table config to tell the space about the new score table — on first write the table is created from the record’s field types.
    2 score.player is a plain str — no FK declaration; the correspondence to person.name is purely by shared value.
    3 second score row.
    4 find all scores for 'marko' — =?= (where) rewrites to SELECT * FROM score WHERE player = 'marko'; the join is computed on demand.
    5 the p.name = s.player join expressed in native SQL; both paths return the same result, the first stays in mtron.

    An auto_from (!*) pointer embedded in a row cell is metatron’s structural foreign key. Unlike a matching column value, the pointer encodes the full address of the linked row directly in the data. auto_from is lazy — the cell stores the unresolved pointer until a path traversal forces it.

    mtron> @/sys/space/lite >>= [table=>[person,score,award]];     ​1​
mtron> lite:award/1 -> [trophy=>'gold',   recipient=>!*lite:person/1]     ​2​
==>[
    trophy=>'gold',
    recipient=>!*lite:person/1]
mtron> lite:award/2 -> [trophy=>'silver', recipient=>!*lite:person/2]     ​3​
==>[
    trophy=>'silver',
    recipient=>!*lite:person/2]
mtron> *lite:award/+     ​4​
==>[
    trophy=>'gold',
    recipient=>!*lite:person/1]
==>[
    trophy=>'silver',
    recipient=>!*lite:person/2]
mtron> *lite:award/1/recipient     ​5​
==>[name=>'marko',age=>29]
    1 extend the table list to include award — each new table is declared before its first write.
    2 recipient stores a !*lite:person/1 pointer — the FK is part of the row value, not a schema constraint.
    3 second award row linking to lite:person/2.
    4 reading all awards — each recipient cell renders as an unresolved !*lite:person/N pointer.
    5 traversing the FK: the path segment recipient forces auto_from to read lite:person/1 and return the full person row.

    The award => person examples above are intra-space references — both tables live under the same lite: pattern. An auto_from pointer can also cross space boundaries, linking a tblespace row directly to an obj in another space. Below, a memspace holds a venue rec, and a lite:venue row carries a !*area:V/1 pointer in its location cell an inter-space reference.

    mtron> memspace::[pattern => area:#]@/sys/space/area     ​1​
mtron> area:V/1 -> [label=>'downtown',capacity=>5000]     ​2​
==>[label=>'downtown',capacity=>5000]
mtron> @/sys/space/lite >>= [table=>[person,score,award,venue]];     ​3​
mtron> lite:venue/1 -> [name=>'main_stage',location=>!*area:V/1,parent=>none]     ​4​
==>[name=>'main_stage',location=>!*area:V/1]
mtron> *lite:venue/1     ​5​
==>[name=>'main_stage',location=>!*area:V/1]
mtron> *lite:venue/1/location     ​6​
==>[label=>'downtown',capacity=>5000]
    1 a memspace at area: provides the inter-space target — any space type (grphspace, fsspace, etc.) works the same way.
    2 writing the venue vertex into area: memspace — this is the row that !*area:V/1 will target.
    3 patch the table config to register the new venue table.
    4 location stores an inter-space !*area:V/1 pointer — the area: prefix is preserved in storage, not rewritten to lite:.
    5 reading the venue row — location renders as the unresolved !*area:V/1 pointer with its original scheme intact.
    6 traversing the inter-space foreign key reference and location forces auto_from to read area:V/1 from the memspace and returns the full vertex record.

    A single row can mix both reference styles. A self column demonstrates an intra-space self-referencing foreign key pointing back at the row’s own table.

    mtron> lite:venue/2 -> [name=>'side_hall',location=>!*g:V/1,parent=>!*lite:venue/1]     ​1​
==>[
    name=>'side_hall',
    location=>!*g:V/1,
    parent=>!*lite:venue/1]
mtron> *lite:venue/2     ​2​
==>[
    name=>'side_hall',
    location=>!*area:V/1,
    parent=>1]
mtron> *lite:venue/2/parent/location     ​3​
==>1
    1 venue/2 mixes inter-space (g:V/1) and intra-space referencing (lite:venue/1) pointers in one row.
    2 both references render unresolved: !*g:V/1 and !*lite:venue/1.
    3 two-hop traversal: parent resolves to venue/1, then location resolves to g:V/1 — inter-space resolution chains.

    foreign key internals

    A tblespace has two storage layers for foreign key pointers. The cell value itself is a plain INTEGER column — no REFERENCES clause, no proprietary SQL type. Routing metadata, column types, and table-level type identity live in a single companion table.

    The _mtron_meta Table
    CREATE TABLE _mtron_meta (
    table_name  VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL,
    column_name VARCHAR(255) NOT NULL,
    base_vid    VARCHAR(128) NOT NULL,
    obj_tid     VARCHAR(512),
    ref_table   VARCHAR(512),
    PRIMARY KEY (table_name, column_name)
);

    Every column touched by metatron gets a row. base_vid stores the base type (/m/uri, /m/int, /m/str, …​) and drives SQL DDL. obj_tid stores the full mtron type refinement (/m/math/nat, /m/lst[uri], …​) and is used at read time to faithfully reconstruct value types without having to rely on heuristic guessing. ref_table is NULL unless the column is a foreign key.

    table_name column_name base_vid obj_tid ref_table

    award

    $table

    /m/rec

    /m/rec

    NULL

    award

    trophy

    /m/str

    /m/str

    NULL

    award

    recipient

    /m/int

    /m/inst/auto_from

    person

    venue

    location

    /m/int

    /m/inst/auto_from

    area:V

    venue

    parent

    /m/int

    /m/inst/auto_from

    venue

    The $table sentinel row (column_name = '$table') records the table/collection-level tid, captured from the rec written on first-insert. This lets the schema instset infer e.g. person::T instead of a generic rec::T, without requiring an explicit type declaration.

    Intra- and Inter-Space Discrimination

    On write, the cell uri is tested against the space’s pattern:

    ref_uri.matches(*space>>pattern)  [-- true → intra-space, store bare table name in _mtron_meta --]
                                  [-- false → inter-space, store scheme:segment in _mtron_meta --]

    • lite:person/1.matches(lite:#") returns true (same scheme, pattern # matches all).

    • area:V/1.matches(lite:#) returns false (scheme mismatch: area vs lite).

    On read, the ref_table value is checked for a colon:

    *ref_table.has(':') [-- true → inter-space, parse as full uri and extend with foreign key id --]
                    [-- false → intra-space, reconstruct from space pattern --]

    • an intra-space ref_table ?= person is combined with the space pattern to produce lite:person/1.

    • an inter-space ref_table ?= area:V is parsed as g:V/1 — the area: scheme routes through the metatron router.

    sql footprint

    The foreign key column is a bare INTEGER (or TEXT for string keys). There are no SQL FOREIGN KEY constraints and the _mtron_meta table is the only metatron-specific schema artifact. If the database is queried by external SQL tooling, FK columns appear as ordinary integers, _mtron_meta is a readable lookup table, and column types are self-documenting via base_vid/obj_tid — nothing is opaque or encoded.

    The Boot Loader

    • Core Structures
    • Boot Structures
    • Boot Loader

    The boot process for metatron loads a few core components and then hands control over to a user-provided boot script. What the user’s boot script does determines the state and use of a particular metatron instance.

    Core Structures

    There are four core structures that are required for metatron to function properly.

    1. /m instset: the core instruction set defining the core types — bool, int, rec, etc. It also includes approximately 200 instructions specifically oriented at the core types.

    2. /m/mach instset: the virtual machine that evaluates mtron code, handles thread management, exposes physical machine resources such as the file system and network, and manages each thread’s /m/space/stack (local variable frames).

    3. /sys/router space: a fundamental component that marshalls read/write requests across spaces both inter- and intra- machine. The router indexes spaces and provides namespace services such as routes and redirects.

    4. /sys space: a /m/space/mem in-memory space necessary for hosting the aforementioned components.

    mtron> */sys/+
==>fail::[apply failure:
   	[lhs]    │ noobj
   	 \_type  │ noobj{0}
   	  \_pred │ []
   	[inst]   │ */sys/+
   	 \_dom   │ #{?}::T
   	 \_args  │ [/sys/+][Space$Helper<255>:Cannot invoke "studio.phaseshift.metatron.isa.m.type.Obj.isPoly()" because the return value of "studio.phaseshift.metatron.util.Tuple$Pair.get1()" is null[Space$Helper<255>:Cannot invoke "studio.phaseshift.metatron.isa.m.type.Obj.isPoly()" because the return value of "studio.phaseshift.metatron.util.Tuple$Pair.get1()" is null] ← Cannot invoke "studio.phaseshift.metatron.isa.m.type.Obj.isPoly()" because the return value of "studio.phaseshift.metatron.util.Tuple$Pair.get1()" is null]][Cannot invoke "studio.phaseshift.metatron.isa.m.type.Obj.isPoly()" because the return value of "studio.phaseshift.metatron.util.Tuple$Pair.get1()" is null[Space$Helper<255>:Cannot invoke "studio.phaseshift.metatron.isa.m.type.Obj.isPoly()" because the return value of "studio.phaseshift.metatron.util.Tuple$Pair.get1()" is null]][Cannot invoke "studio.phaseshift.metatron.isa.m.type.Obj.isPoly()" because the return value of "studio.phaseshift.metatron.util.Tuple$Pair.get1()" is null]@/sys/fail/40
    		 
       ┌───────────────────────┐
   │ write boot/+ with argv│
   └───────────┬───────────┘

    Boot Structures

    The boot process maintains provides access to some information that can be useful during the boot process. Note that boot/ exists under the purview of /m/space/stack (patterned <+/#>).

    1. The system arguments (argv) are stored in boot/args.

    2. The boot loader file reference is stored at boot/file.

    3. Other "workspace" related data may also find its way into boot/+.

    The environmental variables of the underlying operating system are available at /sys/env/+/.

    mtron> */sys/env/+/.take(10)
==>/sys/env/GTK3_MODULES=>'xapp-gtk3-module'
==>/sys/env/BUN_INSTALL=>'/home/killswitch/.bun'
==>/sys/env/JAVA_HOME=>'/home/killswitch/.sdkman/candidates/java/current'
==>/sys/env/QT_IM_MODULE=>'ibus'
==>/sys/env/GNOME_DESKTOP_SESSION_ID=>'this-is-deprecated'
==>/sys/env/PATH=>'/home/killswitch/.local/bin:/home/killswitch/.bun/bin:/home/killswitch/.npm-global/bin:/usr:/opt/rocm-6.4.0/bin:/opt/rocm/bin:/home/killswitch/.opencode/bin:/home/killswitch/.sdkman/candidates/tornadovm/current/bin:/home/killswitch/software/miniconda3/bin:/home/killswitch/.sdkman/candidates/tornadovm/current/bin:/home/killswitch/.sdkman/candidates/maven/current/bin:/home/killswitch/.sdkman/candidates/jbang/current/bin:/home/killswitch/.sdkman/candidates/java/current/bin:/home/killswitch/.local/bin:/home/killswitch/.elan/bin:/home/killswitch/.cargo/bin:/home/killswitch/.local/bin:/usr/local/sbin:/usr/local/bin:/usr/sbin:/usr/bin:/sbin:/bin:/usr/games:/usr/local/games:/snap/bin:/home/killswitch/.lmstudio/bin:/home/killswitch/software/ijhttp:/home/killswitch/.surrealdb:/home/killswitch/.lmstudio/bin:/home/killswitch/go/bin'

==>/sys/env/USER=>'killswitch'

    Boot Loader

    An mtron file can be provided at startup which is used to configure the user’s metatron environment. This typically includes mounting spaces (data access points), importing requisite instruction sets (domains of discourse), configuring q processors (documentation endpoints, etc.), defining custom instructions, and putting the system into a console REPL mode or as a headless server only. An example boot.mtron is provided below.

    [== metatron example boot ==];
print("""
 ____    ___    ___   ______    ___ ___  ______  ____    ___   ____
|    \  /   \  /   \ |      |  |   T   T|      ||    \  /   \ |    \
|  o  )(     YY     )|      |  | _   _ ||      ||  D  )(     )|  _  )
|     T|  O  ||  O  |l_j  l_j  |  \_/  |l_j  l_j|    / |  O  ||  |  |
|  O  ||     ||     |  |  | __ |   |   |  |  |  |    \ |     ||  |  |
|     |l     !l     !  |  ||  T|   |   |  |  |  |  .  \l     !|  |  |
l_____j \___/  \___/   l__jl__jl___j___j  l__j  l__j\_j \___/ l__j__j
""")
print("{{r}}------------\n{{g}}*{{y}}boot/args{{X}}: ",
      *boot/args,
     "\n{{r}}------------{{X}}\n");
[== mount instruction sets ==]
print("{{y}}mounting instruction sets\n{{X}}");
import(/m/mach/io);
import(/m/math,math:);
import(/m/web,web:);
import(/m/iot);
import(/m/iot/haos);
import(/m/iot/miot);
import(/m/grph);
import(/m/llm);
import(/m/tble);
import(/m/dcmnt);
[== store global settings in boot ==]
*<boot/script>.else(boot/script ->
 [sh     => /bin/sh,
  bash   => /bin/bash,
  zsh    => /bin/zsh,
  python => /usr/bin/python3,
  perl   => /usr/bin/perl,
  mtron  => /bin/mtron]),"\n");
[== mount computing spaces ==];
mem::[pattern   => /usr/#,
      q         => [subq::[=>]]]@/sys/space/usr;

      persist   => </tmp/shared.mtron>]@/sys/space/shared;
http::[host     => <http://localhost:8777>,
       pattern  => http://#,

                    /docs => target/docs ]]@/sys/space/www;
graphdb::[pattern  => /h/#,
      route        => [</h/+/>=><>],
      native       => [factory  => mfactory::[=>],
                       load     => grateful]]@/sys/space/grateful;
graphdb::[pattern => g:#,
          route   => [<g:V>=><V>,
                      <g:E>=><E>,
                      <g:S>=>/m/grph/schema/modern],
        native  => [factory  => mfactory::[=>],
                     load     => modern]]@/sys/space/modern;
mqtt::[pattern => <mqtt:#>,
       host    => !*boot/args/mqtt/broker,
       route   => [mqtt:=><>]]@/sys/space/mqtt;
miot::[pattern    => <mtron:#>,
       host       => !*boot/args/mqtt/broker,
       serializer => !*</m/mach/io/serializer/json/simple>,
       route      => [mtron:=>metatron/]]@/sys/space/mqtt/metatron;
mqtt::[pattern    => <z2m:#>,
       host       => !*boot/args/mqtt/broker,

haos::[pattern       => <ha:#>,

                          to(a).map(z2m:).mult(*a>>friendly_name).mult(set/state)

fs::[pattern  => local:#,
      route   => [local:=><~/>],
      script  => !*boot/script]@/sys/space/fs;
serial::[pattern => <serial:#>,
         route   => [serial:=><>]]@/sys/space/serial;
catalog::[host    => <http://localhost:11434>,
          pattern => <ollama:#>,

          route   => [ollama:=><>]]@/sys/space/ollama;
